run_spikespeak.bat --no-browser produced a modal Windows dialog — "The code execution cannot proceed because nicaiu.dll was not found" — a process blocked forever, nothing in output.log, and no ports. nicaiu.dll is the NI-DAQmx runtime. This box has no NI hardware and no NI-DAQmx, and a OneBox-only rig has no reason to.

NIDAQMX.lib is a plain import lib, so linking it made nicaiu.dll a load-time dependency: the loader killed the process before main() ran, which is why nothing could report it. -DSP_ENABLE_NIDAQ=OFF already existed as an escape hatch, but it is a compile-time opt-out that yields a different binary per machine — the requirement was that it should simply run anyway.

Now detected at runtime. /DELAYLOAD:nicaiu.dll + delayimp (root CMakeLists.txt) resolve the DLL on first use instead of at load, and src/modules/nidaq/NiDaqRuntime.{hpp,cpp} probes for it with a cached LoadLibraryA. NIDAQSource::scanForDataSource() asks before the first DAQmx call and declines cleanly; a __pfnDliFailureHook2 backstop names the cause in output.log if a future call site forgets to ask. One binary now runs on both rigs.

Verified in both directions: dumpbin /DEPENDENTS shows nicaiu.dll moved out of the imports into "delay load dependencies"; the backend starts (HTTP 200 on :8234); and selecting NI-DAQ only (real) fails cleanly with "NI-DAQmx runtime not installed; this source cannot be used on this machine" while the app stays up. Every DAQmx call site was audited: all are inside scanForDataSource() (guarded) or an NIDAQDevice method, and those objects are only constructed after the guard passes.

51.2 Machine-specific assumptions that bit, and what changed#

what outcome
Chrome hardcoded in tests/gui/_harness.py Now discovered: Chrome, Brave, Chromium, Edge across %ProgramFiles%/(x86)/%LOCALAPPDATA%, then shutil.which, plus a SPIKESPEAK_TEST_BROWSER override and a failure message listing every path tried. Any Chromium works — the harness only speaks CDP.
The original box's user path C:\Users\BIOEl\Documents\Code\SpikesPeak in 4 scripts All derive from __file__ now. benchmarks/cdp_bench.py was an os.chdir() — an instant crash anywhere else.
HDF5 Installed here as 2.2.0, not 1.14.6, and find_package(HDF5) does not find it — configure fails until HDF5 joins Qt on CMAKE_PREFIX_PATH. The vendored runtime is already the 2.x set, so 2.x is what the build expects; the docs table said 1.14.6 and has been corrected.
MSVC 2019 Not installed. Qt's msvc2019_64 kit builds fine with the VS2022 toolset (cl 19.44), zero warnings. The build dir keeps the documented name — it refers to the Qt kit, not the compiler — so every documented command works verbatim. rebuild.bat already probed both.
python on PATH The WindowsApps alias stub, so python scripts/run_gui_tests.py fails. run_spikespeak.bat already handles this (it probes by executing, then falls back to miniconda) — the GUI runner does not.
.gitignore output.*.log and gui_test_status.json were inert rules: comments had been spliced onto the pattern lines, and .gitignore has no trailing-comment syntax, so output.*.log # ... was one literal pattern matching nothing. Rewritten.

51.3 A red C++ suite names the suite but not the failure#

Proved by injecting QVERIFY2(false, ...) into test_coretypes: the runner correctly reported 1 OF 15 SUITES FAILED, and printed no failing-case name, message or file:line. The Qt Test executables emit zero bytes when stdout is a pipe on this machine (checked from Git Bash, PowerShell and subprocess; they are built /subsystem:console, and -o <file>,txt writes the full log fine), so ctest --output-on-failure has nothing to show. The red light works; diagnosis starts from zero. Suggested fix: give each target -o <name>.log,txt and have run_tests.py print it on failure.

51.4 Results#

C++ suite: green, unchanged. 15 suites / 316 cases — the documented count, verified independently by per-suite file loggers — on a clean configure+build with zero compiler warnings. The most portable part of the stack.

GUI suite: 5 of 6, stream_options red — for the documented reason, confirmed. Running it standalone PASSES, and the contrast pins the cause: under the runner the NP2.0 source was handed ['OneBox ADC (SDR)', 'AP Band', 'LFP Band'] — the previous source's stream list — where standalone correctly offers ['RAW Band']. Labels degrade from 19R/e19 to 19/CH 19 because ChartColumn.js:581 useDepth goes false when the probe ordering never re-applies. That is #51, not a new fault, and the test was left alone.

OneBox NP2.0 multishank — validated end to end on an independent rig. OneBox serial 23190202, FTDI D3XX 1.3.0.10. Connect (Found Neuropixels 2.0 Multi-Shank Probe, virtual slot 16), stream (3954 frames / 126.1 MB in 60 s, 66 f/s, matching the 12 s rate), record (raw + adc .bin with sane sidecars: 30000 Hz / 30300 Hz, adc_int16), reconnect, clean teardown. Six connect/disconnect cycles, six Released virtual slot 16 + connection closed (setSyncAsInput + closeProbe + closeBS + unmapBS), and zero did not return within 3000 ms.

The PXIe profile correctly refuses on this box — No PXI basestation on the bus. 1 device(s) enumerated, none of them PXI — it sees the OneBox and rejects it for a PXIe profile, which is right.

Ripple detection runs on the real probe (stim never armed — no Pi on this rig). Electrodes resolve e100 -> ch196, e200 -> ch248, e300 -> ch60, baselines train to completion, and the per-channel thresholds come out different (mu+5sigma = 9.8 / 16.4 / 11.1) — i.e. estimated from real per-channel noise, not a constant.

51.5 The OneBox ADC opens, and its values are still unverified#

The real source enables the ADC successfully and reads the conversion factor back from the device (lsb_to_voltage = 0.000152588 V, bit_depth = 16), with no np_ADC_enableProbe failure — but it reports this only to the log. The simulated source emits a guiStatus "Found simulated OneBox ADC"; the real one emits no equivalent, so an operator sees the probe confirmed and the ADC not. Worth emitting the same line.

With nothing patched into the BNCs all 12 channels sit at ~1.37 V; four rail across the full +-5 V, and eight swing 1.7 V while resolving only ~40 distinct ADC codes in 246,750 samples (the simulated ADC, whose recorder config is byte-for-byte identical, resolves 15,000-60,000). Ruled out: duplication and de-interleaving — all 12 channels are sample-wise distinct in both sim and real, and no shifted-copy relationship exists (best 48.6% at lag +1, not ~100%). So this is an open question, not a defect claim: settling it needs a known signal on a BNC, which this rig cannot provide. Recommended on a rig that can: drive a 1 Hz / 1 V sine into ADC0 and one SDR channel and confirm amplitude, frequency and channel mapping.

51.6 Not reproducible on this configuration#

No Raspberry Pi at 192.168.1.100, so closed-loop stimulation is untested here (detection only). data/ is absent, so no synthetic-ripple play file and therefore no detections against a known signal. No portrait monitor, so the vertical half of the layout matrix measures a window nobody uses — and running the GUI suite here silently overwrote the committed vertical doc screenshot with a landscape capture (reverted). This OneBox carries the NP2.0 probe, so NP1.0-on-OneBox remains untested.

52. CONFIGURATION 4 ON THE ORIGINAL RIG — AND FOUR ISSUES WORKED TO CONCLUSION (2026-08-21)#

Hardware: NP2.0 multishank on PXIe slot 3, NI-DAQ present, no OneBox attached, probe floating (no saline, no signal), Raspberry Pi stim server reachable. This is configuration 4 of the five in #52; the second workstation had validated configuration 5 earlier the same day.

52.1 What passed#

check result
OneBox profiles refuse with no OneBox deviceReady:false + guiStatus hardware-error — confirmed state, not requested
PXIe connect 8.5 s, repeatable
PXIe streaming 484–582 binary frames / ~35.8 MB per 10–12 s
PXIe record + sidecar 7.20 MB / 20 s; band=raw nch=3 rate=30000 ch=[1,192,196]
PXIe disconnect → reconnect, in session three cycles, 388/387/387 frames, stable connect times
Teardown Neuropixels connection closed, zero bounded-call timeouts, zero warnings

52.2 #54 — two failed OneBox connects kill the next PXIe acquisition (OPEN)#

A genuine 0xC0000005, confirmed by running SpikesPeak.exe directly; the launcher goes through scripts/spikespeak.py, which was swallowing the exit code and making it look like a clean shutdown. The log's last line is the main thread's acquisition confirmed: true — the worker dies on its first read, with no teardown, which on this hardware is the exact state the safety rules exist to avoid.

The trigger is the count of failed OneBox setups — two — and is independent of probe type and of a 20 s gap between them. Zero or one attempt always streams.

Five candidate causes were each implemented, built and re-tested, and each left the crash unchanged: np_closeBS on a PXI slot we never opened; the OneBox teardown firing at an unopened slot; np_checkFtdiDriver per attempt; the whole releaseForeignBasestations body; and timing. After all of it a failed attempt reduces to np_scanBS + np_getDeviceList and still poisons the next acquisition — which points into the vendor driver, past where this codebase can see. Best remaining hypothesis: repeated np_scanBS() with no intervening np_openBS (the PXIe path always opens and closes between scans, and survives).

The elimination work still produced real hardening, kept in 8d93c68: never close hardware this process did not open. Three places were calling vendor close/teardown routines against resources never acquired, with SUCCESS and "not open" codes making that look free.

52.3 #55 — a hardcoded audio device blocked acquisition entirely (FIXED)#

NpxPxiNiDaq.yaml and Npx2PxiNiDaqAsap.yaml pinned device_name: "Speakers (2" — a truncated endpoint name from the original box. Nothing matched here, AudioProcessor::configure() returned false, ProcessorManager discarded six correctly-built processors, and the source never connected. A missing speaker stopped neural acquisition.

It was required in two places — validateConfig() demanded the keys, and AudioWorker::configure() failed hard when the named device was missing — so both had to change. The device is now discovered (PortAudio default) with an explicit name as an optional override, and no audio failure is fatal: the -1 sentinel disables spike audio and acquisition proceeds. slAwStartAudioStream() already degraded this way; configure() was the outlier. Verified: the profile that timed out now connects in 8.5 s with Audio output device: LG HDR WFHD (NVIDIA High Defini via MME (system default) — audio working, not skipped.

52.4 #53 — sidecars now describe the frame (trap FIXED, verification still open)#

num_channels counts the payload. An NPX frame is [uint32 ts][uint16 sync][int16 x N] = N + 3 int16. A reader taking num_channels as the stride mis-de-interleaves everything — and because gcd(12,15) = 3, the columns come out sample-wise distinct but statistically identical, which passes the obvious duplicate-channel check. The timestamp low word read as analog gives a full-rail swing and ~35,000 distinct codes; the high word ~47; sync ~2. Those were exactly the three "anomalous" groups in #53.

The layout was already documented — in the comment above writeMetadataSidecar() — just nowhere a reader could see it. It now ships in every sidecar as a frame block. Verified on a 15 s NP2.0 recording: frame.bytes: 12, file size divides evenly, filesize/12/30000 = 15.00 s. The old reading claims 30.00 s for the same file.

Older recordings are unchanged and still need the stride worked out by hand.

52.5 #56 — filed, then retracted as a test error#

Reported as "PXIe disconnect never completes". It was not. The command is "Disconnect" (capital D) and requires a value; the test sent {"command":"disconnect"}, which matched no branch and was silently dropped. Every symptom followed: nothing ran, no events were emitted, and the later selectSource was correctly refused because the source was still connected. Retested with the real command — three clean cycles.

Two lessons worth keeping. First, the Arming PXI slot 3… line read as a stalled disconnect actually came from the pause a moment earlier; the meaningful signal was the absence of any disconnect log line, not the presence of that one. Second, #54 was checked against controls before filing and held; #56 was not, and did not. The check that would have caught it — does the backend acknowledge this command at all — costs one grep.

53. THE DERIVED BANDS BELONG TO THE PROBE, NOT TO THE PROFILE (2026-08-24)#

Four defects reported from one session in front of the GUI, all the same shape: the GUI was allowed to hold state the backend had not confirmed, and the backend was allowed to leave a dependency implicit. Fixing them exposed a fifth that was considerably worse than any of them.

53.1 What was reported#

"the controls are pinned even when source is disconnected. Also the LFP and Spikeband options were both enabled (green) when I disconnected from the probe and reconnected to the probe with another config file but neither of them had the opportunity from the controls for me to select spike or lfp band."

Measured before touching anything, with no source connected:

colOptions: all "Waiting for data..."        <- controls for a stream that does not exist
lfpBtn:   {shown:true, on:false}
spikeBtn: {shown:true, on:true}              <- green, for a processor from a source that was gone

53.2 The bands are a property of the probe, not of the YAML#

A 2.0 probe has no hardware ap/lfp split. One 30 kHz raw stream; the LFP and spike bands exist only because SpikesPeak derives them. So on a 2.0 source they are not an optional extra a profile opts into — they are the only way to see either band at all, and a profile written before they existed offered no way to look at them.

ProcessorManager::setupProcessors now auto-provisions proc.lfp and proc.spike for any source with data.npx.raw and no data.npx.lfp, and binds them to the display so the per-column dropdown can offer them. 1.0 is deliberately excluded — it streams real hardware bands, and deriving a second copy is a different feature with a different justification.

Declared is not running. mEnabled used to default true, so merely listing proc.lfp meant every 2.0 session filtered all 384 channels through a band nobody had asked to see, with the button green before the operator touched anything. A band now starts off and runs when one of three things happens: the operator presses its button, something that needs it turns it on, or the profile says enabled: true. Nothing shipped records a derived band, so nothing records silence as a result — and that is by design, since a derived band is the raw band plus documented coefficients and is reproduced offline exactly.

53.3 Why the button was green for a source that was gone#

The processor registry hands out one instance per name for the life of the process, so "the object exists" says nothing about the profile in front of you. WebSocketServer was keying the band relay off getProcessorByName(...) != nullptr — and that call lazily creates, so it was true on every profile ever.

  • BandFilterProcessor::isProvisioned() — set by configure(), cleared by ProcessorManager on teardown. The relay keys off that, and announces directly rather than by a queued getState, because processor threads do not start until Play and the buttons would otherwise stay hidden until then — on a control whose whole purpose is to be set before streaming.
  • The browser drops its band and audio state on a source change and waits to be told again.

53.4 The dependency, enforced where it belongs#

On a 2.0 probe the ripple detector reads data.lfp. That was a comment in a config file and a warning line in the GUI. It is now enforced in the backend, both directions:

direction behaviour
start detection with the band off RippleDetector::ensureInputBandEnabled() switches proc.lfp on — on GUI start, on the legacy enable, and on auto_start, so a headless run gets the same guarantee. The band announces its own state; the button turns green because the backend did it.
disable the band while detection runs a blocking dialog naming both consequences. Confirming stops detection first, then disables the band. Cancelling changes nothing.

The detector reports its bound band in rippleState ("input":"data.lfp"), so the GUI decides from what the backend says rather than from a guess about probe type — which also handles a 1.0 profile deliberately pointed at a derived band, where a probe-type test would be wrong.

53.5 THE ONE THAT MATTERED — a re-enabled band was silently stale#

Found by an adversarial review of the diff, not by any test that existed.

BandFilterProcessor used one counter for two roles: the read cursor into data.npx.raw, and the write slot into its own output ring. A disabled band advances the first and commits nothing, so they diverge by exactly the length of the disabled stretch — while every consumer indexes the ring by its own committed-sample count (constDataAt is ind % capacity).

That was correct only for a band running from the first sample, which is what every profile used to do. §53.2 turned it into the default path. The producer would resume writing at skipped % capacity while consumers still read from committed % capacity, and the band would then serve correct-looking samples of the wrong age: up to a second of extra delay at 30 kHz, different every session, on a closed loop this repo defends in single milliseconds. Nothing throws, nothing logs, and no test that checks "is data flowing" would see it.

The write slot now follows the commit count. tests/test_bandfilterprocessor.cpp (new, 17th suite) pins it and was proven able to fail by restoring the single counter.

53.6 A refused source select reported itself as a switch#

GuiTestCoverage.md §4a recorded this as "once acquisition has run, switching to another source fails". That diagnosis was wrong, and the WebSocket experiment that settles it takes two minutes: disconnect first and the switch after streaming works perfectly. What actually happens is that handleConnectRequest refuses a connect while a source is still selected — correct behaviour — while onSourceSelection had already broadcast stateSource naming the requested source. The GUI's own picker sends selectSource with no Disconnect, so this is the ordinary operator path.

The picker therefore showed the new source while the OLD source's streams kept flowing; a recording taken in that state would carry the wrong band. Now the refusal restores the source that actually holds, re-asserts deviceReady, hands the module state back, and says "Still connected to X. Disconnect before selecting another source" instead of diagnosing hardware that is fine.

53.7 ASAP mode on the derived bands#

Simulated NP2.0, 384 ch, same profile with and without low_latency: true, 35 one-second windows:

mode LFP backlog Spike backlog commit batch
normal 1.002 ms 1.002 ms 30.1 samples
ASAP 0.038 ms 0.037 ms 1.1 samples

ASAP takes ~1.00 ms off both bands — 26× — and the two stay identical to each other. The batch column is the mechanism in plain sight: the backlog is the source's commit granularity, not the filtering, so changing the granularity moves it and nothing else (filter_us_per_sample unchanged at 1.3–1.7 µs). Under ASAP the band totals are ~0.66 ms (LFP) and ~0.11 ms (spike) — almost entirely the filter's own group delay, which is the floor.

53.8 A missing audio device stopped acquisition#

Pa_Initialize() failing made AudioProcessor::configure() return false; ProcessorManager treats a failed configure as fatal and abandons the whole graph. So a machine with no speakers could not connect a probe on any profile listing proc.audio. The profile was fine; the speakers were missing.

configure() now separates a wrong profile (validateConfig — still fatal) from a missing device (degraded): the processor stays on the graph, reports ready (sources do not start until every processor has), ticks so the liveness monitor is not staring at a processor that never turns, and answers available:false with an unavailable_reason the panel shows. Verified by forcing Pa_Initialize to fail: 1713 frames streamed in 12 s with audio unavailable. Closes the last open item on #55.

53.9 Housekeeping this turned up#

  • The GUI harness never disconnected before selecting, and a connect while connected is refused quietly. It worked by accident — the backend auto-quits ~2 s after its last client leaves — until a test connected inside that window, reused a backend that still had a source, and ran against the previous test's source. Twelve unrelated failures about labels and units.
  • BandFilterProcessor.cpp included <QApplication>, pulling Qt Widgets into a signal-processing translation unit, for a symbol it did not use. It does use QCoreApplication::processEvents(), which is Core.
  • developer_docs/AgentTraps.md is new — the things here that are not wrong but are not what they look like, written from time actually lost. kHeadstageSamplingRate is 100 kHz ticks, not the 30 kHz sample rate (cost two red runs against correct code); getDataByName creates what you asked it to check for; a queued call to a processor is not delivered until Play; destroying a running QThread reports as a test with no output at all.
  • CLAUDE.md gained "What this system is optimising for" — real time, minimal overhead, no new dependencies, nothing running that nobody asked for, and SciChart explicitly on notice for removal.

54. THE TEST SUITE GROWS TEETH, AND THE AUDIO FINDS ITS WAY OUT (2026-08-24, overnight)#

Four operator instructions drove this, and each one turned into a defect rather than a feature:

"for the GUI then it seems like you're not testing every possible case ... shouldn't you also add in error checks to the GUI testing suite? I realize this makes it run longer which is okay but be thorough."

"there are typically going to be multiple audio output things available per computer ... the audioprocessor needs to be able to adapt to whatever the user changed the audioplayback output device to in real-time"

"For Audio button just have the audio emoji makes it smaller. You probably need the 'bands' button and a drop down"

"given that more things are added to the GUI the shrinking should set wider width limit probably to pass"

54.1 The width limit did not need widening — and finding that out found a bug#

toolbar_layout ran only on the harness default, which is a 1.0 source. A 1.0 source has no derived bands, so the toolbar under test was missing the Bands button entirely: the 1080 px floor had been established against the fewest buttons the application can display. It now sweeps both toolbars:

toolbar modules folds into 1080 px
1.0 (light) ripple 2 rows, tb-peek-view from 1280
2.0 (heavy) Bands, audio, ripple identical

So the floor stays at 1080, measured rather than assumed — consolidating the two band buttons into one paid for the new one.

That sweep printed which module buttons were present at each width, and the 1.0 row read audio,ripple. No shipped 1.0 profile declares proc.audio. The audio button had been on the toolbar of every profile ever selected, controlling a processor that was not on the graph — the identical defect §53.3 fixed for the band buttons, in a module nobody had re-checked. Two causes, both fixed: getProcessorByName() lazily CREATES, so the relay's non-null test was always true; and AudioModule._build() ran on DOMContentLoaded and showed its button regardless of any backend state. isProvisioned() now lives on IProcessor rather than on one subclass, so the next module cannot repeat it.

54.2 Audio now actually follows the output device you choose#

The operator's requirement was real-time adaptation. The reason it did not work is worth stating precisely, because it is not obvious:

Pa_GetDefaultOutputDevice() returns a CONCRETE endpoint. Measured here: index 1, "ASUS VE278 (NVIDIA High Definition Audio)" via MME, on a machine reporting fourteen output devices across MME / DirectSound / WASAPI / WDM-KS. A stream opened on a concrete endpoint keeps playing to that endpoint forever, whatever the operator later selects in Windows.

Index 0 on the same machine was "Microsoft Sound Mapper - Output" — the virtual endpoint that routes to whatever the current default is, and moves the instant it changes. So follow-the-default mode now opens the follower (AudioWorker::findDefaultFollower()), and adaptation costs nothing: no polling, no re-initialisation, no gap in the audio. Falls back to the plain default where no follower exists (macOS), and the match is by name, which is a heuristic and is documented as one.

Also added: the panel enumerates every device with its host API and lets the operator pin one (setDevice on id 0x218, -1 to go back to following); a watchdog notices a stream whose device was unplugged, disabled or taken exclusively, and reopens on whatever the machine offers now — the same path that picks up speakers plugged in after the session started. The watchdog backs off to one attempt per 5 s when there is no device at all, because on a rack-mounted rig that branch is reached on every tick and re-enumerating means Pa_Terminate() + Pa_Initialize().

54.3 Three toolbar buttons became two#

Audio is the glyph alone; LFP and Spike collapsed into one Bands button with a dropdown whose badge counts how many bands are running, not how many exist. It scales: a third derived band is another menu row rather than another permanent fixture on a bar with a hard width floor.

54.4 The GUI suite now catches what it could not see#

Every test asserts the page threw nothing. Gui.check_clean() reads a collector installed via Page.addScriptToEvaluateOnNewDocument — in place before any application script runs — that records uncaught exceptions, unhandled rejections, failed resource loads and console.error. A GUI can look perfectly correct in a screenshot while throwing on every frame and not one layout assertion would notice. Collected in the page rather than off the CDP event stream, because cdp.py's send() loops until it sees its own message id and discards everything else.

Three new suites, all hardware-free:

suite covers
error_paths A refused select, a refused play, a source that cannot load, and controls that must be DISABLED rather than inert. The refused-select case is the regression test §53.6 shipped without.
audio_panel Device enumeration, follow-the-default vs pinned, the picker round trip through the backend, threshold and mute. The panel had no coverage at all.
record_flow Arm to record to stop, and what reached the disk — non-zero .bin sizes and a sidecar whose sample_rate_hz is not 0 and whose band is not a filename. Recording is what this application exists to do and it had no GUI test.

stream_options also gained the 2.0-on-OneBox profile — the closest simulated twin of the rig configuration in use, and the only shipped source that puts a non-384-wide stream (the 12-channel SDR ADC) beside a probe band.

Every new assertion was proven able to fail by injecting the defect it exists to catch and watching it go red: the follower selection, the refused-select restore, the error collector, and the band write-slot fix from §53.5.

54.5 One runner, one page, an ETA#

python scripts/run_all_tests.py runs both suites behind a live page: per-test bars with elapsed-vs-expected, an overall countdown, a streaming tail of the running test's output, and the time remaining in the tab title. The GUI suite used to print nothing until it had finished, so the honest answer to "how long until this is done" was "no idea", for eleven minutes, every time.

Estimates come from what the tests actually took on this machine last time; progress is measured in TIME, not tests completed, because the GUI tests differ by 3x in duration. Standard library only — http.server, threading, subprocess — and the page is plain HTML/CSS/DOM with one fetch loop, per the dependency rule now written into CLAUDE.md.

54.6 A risk this work created, caught before it reached the rig#

Auto-provisioning proc.audio onto every probe profile made a latent bug reachable. AudioWorker returned from three paths -- no output device, Pa_OpenStream failed, Pa_StartStream failed -- without emitting sigAwStarted. MainController does not start the SOURCES until every processor has reported ready, so any of those three would have hung the start.

Before this change only five profiles carried proc.audio, and on a machine where the device opens it never fired. After it, every probe profile carries it. On a rig, during an experiment, that surfaces as "the probe will not start" with the real cause being a sound device.

All three paths now report started. Verified by forcing Pa_OpenStream to fail: the source still connects and streams (578 frames in 12 s, which is exactly one third of the 1734 measured with the bands on -- frames scale with active streams, and bands now default off).

Found by reading the diff for what it had made reachable, not by a test. Worth saying plainly, because the tests added this same night would not have caught it: they all run on a machine whose audio device opens.

Green at the end of the night: 17/17 C++ suites, 10/10 GUI tests (~19 min).

55. THE DAC WAS NEVER SWITCHED ON, AND THE ONEBOX STOPPED STREAMING (2026-08-24, late)#

Issue #49 had been open four days on the theory that the WavePlayer output was going to the wrong connector. It was going nowhere, and the reason was one missing line in our own code.

55.1 arm() never enabled the DAC output#

A DAC channel does not drive its pin until np_DAC_enableOutput(ch, true) is called. DAC_setVoltage() alone changes nothing on the wire, and neither does a correctly loaded and armed WavePlayer buffer. So every call returned SUCCESS, wavePlayerStatus() reported loaded=yes armed=yes, and all twelve ADC channels recorded silence. The status was accurate. The box simply was not driving anything.

Found by a DC sweep rather than by reasoning: a commanded level appeared on its BNC the instant DAC_enableOutput was added, and not before.

55.2 The output is on DAC0 → breakout BNC IO0, not the SMA#

Measured with DAC0 and DAC4 both output-enabled, so a routing surprise would show up somewhere. A 1 Hz ±2 V square, sd per channel over 3 s before vs 5 s during:

IO0    sd 0.0002 -> 1.9894    min -1.992  max +1.998   <== the waveform
IO4    sd 0.0002 -> 0.0001    held at -0.017 V         (DAC4: enabled, idle)
IO9    sd 0.0001 -> 0.0001    4.963 V, unchanged       <== SMA, untouched

IO4/IO6 sitting at −0.017 V rather than the ~1.715 V float of the other idle lines is the control: "enabled" really does mean "driving".

waveplayer_arm is documented as "routing output DAC0 to the SMA output". The first half is right; the SMA half does not happen on this hardware. Do not generalise into a DAC-N → IO-N map — the sweep found DAC4 driving IO4 and IO6 together, which is unexplained.

Two things fell out of the same measurement:

  • Arming does not disturb sync. IO9 carried the 1 Hz sync clock throughout and did not move by an LSB across arm, fire and stop. The teardown-ordering comment claimed a race between waveplayer_stop and setSyncAsInput; they touch different pins.
  • kDefaultCodesPerVolt = 6553.6 is confirmed, twice: a DC sweep read back linear, and the WavePlayer path — the one that matters, since it goes through the raw int16 buffer — returned +1.998/−1.992 for ±2.000 V commanded. "Nominal" is gone from the GUI.

Wording corrected in OneBoxWavePlayer.{hpp,cpp}, OneBoxSource.cpp and OneBoxModule.js, each carrying the measurement inline. #49 closed.

55.3 dac_trigger: both trigger paths from one crossing#

New detector config key (default off). When set, every detection fires the OneBox WavePlayer as well as the stim server, and detect_to_dac_us is recorded beside detect_to_send_us. Both come from the same threshold crossing, which makes it a paired comparison rather than two runs matched afterwards — removing the sync-alignment class of error that cost a session in §24.

The meta-method index is resolved once and cached (a by-name invokeMethod would put a string lookup inside the interval being measured), the invoke is DirectConnection on purpose, and each stage is timed from the detection instant so whichever fires second is not charged for the first.

Verified to initialise on the real rig — DAC trigger armed, stim params -> 60 us phase, 10 us gap, 1 Hz, 1 ms train, ARM stim -> 192.168.1.100:8888. The measurement itself did not run, because:

55.4 The OneBox stopped delivering data, and it is not our code#

Connect succeeds completely — FTDI 1.3.10, device found by serial, headstage auto-detected on port 1, ADC range read back from the device. Then acquisition starts and zero samples arrive, after which a second np_arm() never returns and teardown abandons every remaining vendor call on its timeout.

Bisected with three standalone programs that link only the vendor API:

exercised result
basestation — scanBS/mapBS/openBS/closeBS/unmapBS clean, twice
+ probe — detectHeadStage/openProbe/init/closeProbe clean
+ acquisition — np_arm/np_setSWTrigger/np_readPackets 0 packets; box then dead

np_readPackets returned SUCCESS on every call for three seconds and produced nothing. Tracked as #59; the blocked measurement is #60.

Two traps came out of this and are now in AgentTraps.md:

  • A control-plane probe is not a liveness test. After the first failure the basestation-only sequence came back completely clean and I reported the box as recovered. The next streaming attempt reproduced the fault exactly. The control plane and the data plane fail independently here.
  • The second np_arm() is what hangs. Since closeDataSource() opens with np_arm (close), a dead data plane presents as a teardown hang — pointing the reader at shutdown code, where the bug is not.

The stall watchdog and the bounded vendor calls both did their job: acquisition reporting healthy while zero samples arrived was caught in 3 s, and a wedged basestation did not hang shutdown.

55.5 Two references, written because they were needed this session#

ConfigReference.md — 77 config keys across every source and processor, extracted from the call sites and verified against every shipped profile; where they disagreed the code won. Plus the 48 runtime-only parameters, which is the more useful half: zscore_threshold and lockout_seconds are not config keys, and a profile built around them produces nothing while looking correct.

ClosedLoopDriving.md — the command sequences that work. Use ripple_tools/rig.py; retrain() is mandatory and its absence is indistinguishable from a working detector on a quiet signal; how to tell "no samples" from "trained but quiet" via rippleEnvelope; and output.log lives in the backend's working directory. I lost a run to the missing retrain() this very session, after writing the page that says so.

Green at the end: 17/17 C++ suites, 10/10 GUI tests.

56. ONE ROW IF IT FITS, AND A CONTROL THAT NEVER RAN (2026-08-25)#

Audio device selection, the per-column control strip, and two measurements — one of which failed in a way worth more than the measurement.

56a. The shipped audio default was the slowest path Windows offers#

Pa_GetDefaultHostApi() returns MME. Measured on this rig: MME 99.9 ms of output latency against DirectSound's 8.7 ms on the identical speakers. Nothing chose between them, and the picker listed eighteen entries with no indication that most were the same four outputs reached badly.

hostApiRank() now orders the host APIs by MEASUREMENT rather than by what they advertise — DirectSound promises 120 ms and delivers under 19; WASAPI promises 3 ms, delivers 20, and refuses to open on three of this rig's four outputs. physicalName() groups a speaker across APIs despite MME truncating names to 31 characters. enumerateOutputs() reports physical / api_rank / fastest with an index tie-break, because a "fastest" mark that appears twice tells the operator nothing. findDefaultFollower() picks the follower endpoint by rank, so following the OS setting no longer means MME.

The picker became a button and a themed menu rather than a <select>: a native select's popup is OS-drawn, so the green "fastest" rows rendered green-on-white the moment it opened, and no CSS reaches inside it.

56b. The control strip had two faults with one root#

The per-column band (stream / shank / L+R / µV / density) had three authored shapes chosen by twelve @container tiers keyed on column count. Both halves of (!vertical && nCols > 2) || doesNotFit were wrong, measured on the 1920×1080 display:

case one row needs / has did should
3 columns, dock shut 448 / 477 — fits collapsed behind a chip stay open
2 columns at 1080px 448 / 437 — does not fit stacked to two rows collapse

The second is the interesting one. The tier chose the shape, and the fit test then measured the shape the tier had chosen. At 1080px the band was already stacked, so the test measured 274px, found it fitting in 377px, and never collapsed. A circle that settles happily on a wrong answer and looks green from every angle.

So the in-flow band has exactly ONE shape and ChartColumn.fitStrips() collapses it iff 448px does not fit beside the depth readout. All twelve tiers deleted. Two things the change was not allowed to break, both found by auditing before writing:

  • The decision is made once per row. .column-container is flex:1 1 0 and Chrome distributes the remainder in 1/64px LayoutUnits while clientWidth rounds to an integer, so per-column measurement lets siblings land on opposite sides of the threshold — two header heights in one row, traces starting at different Y. The per-column version also flipped a class N times per frame to measure, each flip a size change the ResizeObserver picked up, which called back in. It took the page down.
  • The collapsed overlay keeps the narrow template. It is position:absolute and set no template, so it inherited the in-flow one. #view-data is overflow:hidden, which CLIPS an absolutely positioned descendant — a 448px overlay in a column too narrow for one would have lost 275px of controls on the last column at 1080 with the dock open.

layout_matrix asserted the OLD rule as a requirement, and its one-line/header-height invariants were gated behind if vertical: — which is exactly why a two-row HORIZONTAL strip ran green for months. They now run on both axes, the rule is asserted in its own operands read from live layout (never a table of expected collapse counts: the suite runs full-screen on whichever monitors are attached), and two new assertions pin the uniformity the shared container query used to give for free.

56c. The throughput shortfall does not reproduce here#

63 (split out of #53's comments, where it had been sitting unowned): the second machine recorded at#

78.1 % of nominal with the ADC enabled and 99.99 % without it, and decayed to 65.8 % by t+90 s regardless. Nothing warned — zero WARN, zero CRITICAL — because the stall watchdog only fires on a total stop.

Run here, four cells, counted from file size over the sidecar's own declared frame width: 100.0 % on every cell, both bands, t+15 s and t+90 s. So it is specific to that machine or its environment, not the code, and the investigation moves to comparing hosts.

56d. …because the control never ran — and I misdiagnosed why#

The four cells alternated between Npx2OneBox and a new Npx2OneBoxNoAdc control. Every profile switch after the first failed to take effect, so all four ran under the first profile: the cells named "no ADC" contain ADC data.

I first attributed this to #51 (source switch fails while the backend announces success) and posted it as a hardware reproduction. That was wrong, and it is retracted on the issue. The backend behaved correctly. My script sent {"command": "disconnect"}; the handler is command == "Disconnect" && obj.contains("value") — capital D, value required — so the message matched nothing and the source was never released. The backend then refused the switch and said why:

19:11:31.028 [MainController]  Error: You must disconnect from a source before attempting to set a new one
19:11:31.029 [WebSocketServer] select "NPX 2.0 Multishank (OneBox, no ADC)" REFUSED
                               -- "NPX 2.0 Multishank (OneBox)" is still connected; state restored

Refused, reason given, state restored — the opposite of #51's signature. #51 stands where it did: reproducible in sim, not observed on hardware.

The lesson is the diagnosis, not the bug. I matched a symptom — "the new profile didn't take and the recording looks normal" — to a known issue, and did not read output.log, which was in the backend's working directory the whole time and answered it in one line. The recorder log I cited was real but described the recorder correctly recording the profile that was correctly still loaded; it said nothing about the cause. Read the backend's own log before attributing a symptom to a filed issue.

The one real finding, and it is small: an unrecognised WebSocket text command produces no diagnostic at all — no warning, no log, no reply. A one-character case error in a command name is silent, and cost a hardware run. Same family as "text frames on a module channel are dropped silently", already in AgentTraps.

56e. Reproducibility#

Both latency write-ups ended with a "Reproducing" section naming a script that existed only in a scratchpad. ripple_tools/measure_av_latency.py and ripple_tools/fair_ab.py are now in the repo with paths derived from __file__. An audit of every python <path>.py in developer_docs/ and RecordingTests/ found two more stale paths; all eighteen now resolve. Npx2OneBoxNoAdc.yaml is committed for the same reason — a control in one machine's build tree is not a control.

feature/SpikeAudio merged into feature/OneBoxSupport and deleted. Issues #61, #62, #63 filed;

60 closed with its results (363 µs, n ≈ 1000 per ordering).#

Green at the end: 18/18 C++ suites, 10/10 GUI tests.

57. A RECORDING THAT CANNOT BE TURNED BACK INTO MICROVOLTS (2026-08-25)#

Issues #28 and #40. The requirement is that every bit of the signal be reconstructible from the recorded data. A sidecar written the night before contained, in full:

band, channels, data_name, filename, frame{...}, interpreter, num_channels,
record_format, recording_name, sample_rate_hz, timestamp, units: "adc_int16"

It declares the samples are ADC counts and offers no way to turn them into volts. The per-channel gain that sets the microvolt scale is fixed by the applied IMRO and lives in the probe amplifiers; it is not recomputable from anything on disk. So a session recorded with a non-default map had a microvolt scale that could not be recovered from the files at all.

Nothing errors in that failure. The traces are real neural data, the file is the right length, the sync is clean, and every microvolt figure derived from it is wrong by the gain ratio. Both consumers assumed the default anyway -- DisplayProcessor.cpp's hardcoded 500/250 and python/spikespeak_postprocess/reader.py's _UV_PER_BIT, the latter of which simply raises for any band that is not NP1 ap/lfp. The next milestone is in-vivo under anesthesia, which is the most expensive data there is to reacquire.

57a. The structural reason it was missing#

RecordProcessor holds no reference to any source, probe or basestation. RecordingInfo is nine fields, every one of them from YAML or the GUI channel override. That is not an oversight in the JSON assembly -- the recorder has nothing to ask.

And it must not acquire one: SourceRegistry::getSourceByName constructs a source that is absent (AgentTraps.md), so reaching for the probe from the recorder would instantiate a second one.

So the dependency is inverted. src/core/RecordingMetadata.hpp is a blackboard modelled directly on ChannelMapService: sources publish an immutable snapshot, the recorder reads it. Same threading contract -- a mutex-guarded shared_ptr, snapshots immutable after publish -- so the recorder's worker thread can read while the GUI mutates the IMRO on the main thread. Keyed by producer rather than single-slot, because a PXIe rig runs a probe and an NI-DAQ at once and each recording has to find its own publisher.

Publishing happens inside publishChannelMap(), so the channel map and the gains can never describe different maps: there is exactly one place a new map becomes current. Built once per map change, read once per record press. Nothing per sample, nothing on the 30 kHz path, no new dependency, and no value the hardware was not already asked for at connect -- four things were being read and thrown away (probe serial and part number, the calibration CSV paths, the OneBox serial, the ADC bit depth), all of them function locals that went to a log line and nowhere else.

A source withdraws on close. A snapshot left standing after a disconnect stamps the next recording with a probe that is gone, which is worse than no metadata because it looks like evidence.

57b. What the file now says#

metadata_version: 2. Every v1 key keeps its name and meaning -- purely additive, so ripple_tools/, python/spikespeak_postprocess and the offline exporters being built in parallel keep parsing these files unchanged.

  • scale -- volts_per_count, microvolts_per_count, gain, uniform, the formula it was derived by, and its provenance.
  • columns -- parallel arrays in file column order: channel, electrode, shank, reference, gain, volts_per_count. index_space: "readout_channel" finally states what channels indexes; the old comments said electrodes, which is true only on NP1 bank 0.
  • acquisition -- probe type/serial/part number, the applied IMRO verbatim, reference selection, basestation, calibration state, ADC range and bit depth, frame_timestamp_hz.
  • frame.timestamp_hz: 100000 -- the frame timestamp is a hardware TICK counter, not a sample index. Read as samples it is 3.33x wrong, which is row 1 of AgentTraps.md.

Provenance is written beside every number, because "the device told us this" and "we assumed the usual default" are not the same evidence and a future reader must be able to tell which one they hold: device-readback, applied-map, compiled-constant, simulated-constant, derived.

A value that is not knowable is an explicit null with a reason, never a plausible default. No publisher, an unrecognised probe type, a OneBox whose ADC range was never applied -- each says so, in its own words, in the file.

Two traps closed on the way:

  • NP1's two bands run at different gains (500 and 250). Writing the AP factor into the LFP sidecar scales every LFP microvolt by exactly 2 -- large enough to matter, small enough to look plausible. Each band carries its own.
  • A mixed-gain map gets no scalar at all. If the recorded columns disagree, volts_per_count is null and columns.volts_per_count carries the truth, so there is no number a reader can pick up that is wrong for some of its columns.
  • The OneBox ADC shares a publisher with the probe and gets no probe geometry. ADC channel 3 is BNC IO3, not the electrode the probe's channel 3 reads. The electrode table is sitting right there when that sidecar is written; attaching it would put a specific, plausible, completely wrong electrode number on every ADC column.

57c. Sim publishes too#

Every simulated source publishes the same blocks, marked simulated-constant. The GUI suite records through sim on every run, so a missing scale block is caught there rather than on in-vivo data -- and the exporters are developed against the shape hardware actually produces. The provenance is what keeps a generated file from ever being mistaken for an acquired one.

57d. Still assumed elsewhere#

DisplayProcessor.cpp:182-189 still hardcodes 500/250 for the live NP1 microvolt scale, and reader.py still carries _UV_PER_BIT. Neither affects what reaches disk any more, but both are wrong for a non-default map. Left alone deliberately: the exporters are being built in parallel and the display change is a behaviour change, not a metadata one.

tests/test_recordingmetadata.cpp, 18 cases, links the real assembler. Falsified three times before being believed: removing the mixed-gain check turns 1 case red, reinstating the "assume gain 500" bug turns 5 red, and dropping the probe-geometry guard turns the ADC case red.

58. THE NUMBERS WERE WRONG AND THE TESTS SAID NOTHING (2026-08-25, overnight)#

Continues §56, after the operator's demo. Four defects, all of one kind: something that looked correct and was not, with no signal that anything was amiss.

57a. NP2.0 microvolts were 19 % too high — and both manuals were in the repo#

Found while validating the new recording converters against ground truth, not by looking for it. SpikesPeak converted NP2.0 counts with 1.0 V / 84 / 4096 = 2.9064 uV/count. imec's own documents — the Neuropixels 2.0 User Manual V1.0.6 p.15 and the NP2.0 probe spec sheet p.3, copies of both sitting in manuals/ the whole time — state:

GAIN 100        INPUT VOLTAGE RANGE 10 mVpp        ADC RESOLUTION 12 bits

So the correct step is 10 000 uV / 4096 = 2.4414 uV/count. The full scale (10 mV x gain 100 = 1.0 V at the ADC) and the code count were already right; only the gain was wrong, and every NP2.0 microvolt figure we have ever produced is 100/84 = 1.190x too large.

It is a constant factor, which is exactly why it survived: relative comparisons, detections, quorum, and every latency figure are untouched. Only absolute microvolts — including the live view's scale, i.e. the number an operator reads and sets a ripple threshold against. The counts were never affected, so recorded data is fully recoverable: divide published NP2.0 microvolt figures by 1.190. NP1.0 is unaffected; the two conventions agree there exactly.

One term the manuals could not settle was decided empirically rather than assumed: whether the 12-bit code sits left-shifted inside the int16, which would make the divisor 8192. Across 300 027 real frames the low bits vary at 0.56 / 0.30 / 0.16 against the 0.50 / 0.25 / 0.125 expected of an unshifted code — a shift would leave them identically zero. 4096 is right.

Issue #65. The constant now has one definition with the citation attached, instead of a bare 84.0f at two sites.

57b. Two tests were green without testing anything#

The GUI suite's only "is anything actually drawn" assertion. tests/gui/live_view.py read c.sciChartSurface; ChartColumn stores this.surface. That property has never existed, so every column returned null — and the assertion accepted null. A renderer that drew nothing would have gone 10/10 green. It now requires a real count, and its failure message names the likelier cause, because "nothing is drawn" and "the probe lost track of the renderer" want different responses. Proved able to fail: restoring the old property name turns it red in all three layouts.

And a correction to a claim made earlier in the session. tests/test_recordingmetadata.cpp was described as always-green because it passed with the provenance work stashed. That was wrong: RecordingMetadata.cpp/.hpp are untracked, and git stash push -- src/ stashes only tracked modifications, so the code under test was never stashed — only its integration. The test is real; a one-byte error in the frame width turns it red.

57c. The C++ suite could not build on Linux at all#

-mavx2;-mfma were attached to the SpikesPeak target only, while test_avx2filter and test_bandfilterprocessor compile src/core/Avx2Filter.cpp directly and spikespeak_test() set no compile options. MSVC accepts AVX2 intrinsics without an architecture flag, so this was invisible here; GCC does not, and it is a hard compile error. "Both suites pass" was therefore unreachable on Linux — the outstanding row of #52, and the main thing gating the merge to develop. Found while writing the Linux runbook, verified against the target definitions, fixed for every test target.

57d. A recording now carries the numbers it was taken with#

Sidecars are metadata_version: 2 and carry per band a scale block (volts_per_count, gain, the formula in words, and the provenance of the number — device-readback / applied-map / config / compiled-constant / simulated-constant) and a columns block (per channel: readout channel, electrode, shank, reference, gain, volts_per_count).

columns.index_space closes a real trap: channels has always been read-out channel indices while the comments and Recording.md called them electrode numbers. On NP1 bank 0 the two coincide, so it read correctly for years; on NP2 the map is a silicon permutation and they differ for nearly every column — a recording that looks perfect, with real data, the right length and clean sync, in which only the depth is a lie.

Additive by contract: every previous key is still written with the same meaning, so ripple_tools/, python/spikespeak_postprocess and both exporters keep parsing unchanged.

57e. Offline converters, and what validating them found#

Open Ephys binary and SpikeGLX exporters (#28, #40), numpy-only, formats read from each tool's own source on this machine and checked against real recordings from both. Gaps survive into Open Ephys sample_numbers; SpikeGLX has no per-sample timestamp, so that converter verifies uniformity and refuses rather than padding — padding would launder a dropped-samples fault into clean-looking data. Both behaviours proved able to fail.

The trap they had to survive: the recorded timestamp is a 100 kHz tick counter, not a sample index — consecutive frames differ by 3 or 4 ticks in a 2:1 ratio, so a uniformity test written as diff == 1 rejects every healthy file.

57f. #53: the ADC verified against a known signal#

Using the loopback already wired (DAC0 → IO0 → cable → IO11), eight commanded DC levels across ±2.5 V:

FIT   counts = 6531.2 * volts + 28.2      max |residual| = 1.9 counts
device-reported (np_ADC_getStreamConversionFactor): 6553.6 counts/V   -> 0.34 % agreement

IO0 and IO11 both respond with spans matching to 0.2 counts; all ten other channels move under one count, which is the part that matters — nothing bleeds across the map, so --stim-channel N in the closed-loop analyses addresses the input it claims to. Idle inputs sit at ~11266 counts ≈ 1.72 V, matching the documented float. A small real offset: +28 counts ≈ 4.3 mV at 0 V, reproduced at both zero points.

57g. #63 and #64, and #51 narrowed#

RecordProcessor now compares records written against elapsed × nominal at record stop, per band, and warns below 95 %. The measured shortfall never reproduced on either rig — the second machine ran old and new code on one box, which is the arm that actually exonerated the code, and corrected a confounded inference of mine in the process — but the blind spot was real: a band at 66 % of nominal logged zero WARN and zero CRITICAL, because the stall watchdog fires on total silence, not a partial deficit.

An unrecognised WebSocket command now logs at WARN instead of vanishing. It vanished before, and a {"command":"disconnect"} where the handler wants Disconnect with a value silently failed to release a source, which cost a four-cell hardware run whose files looked perfect afterwards.

51 was re-run and narrowed, not closed: the steady state is correct, but a refused switch still#

broadcasts the requested source before restoring — a transient the GUI never sees because it reads the latest value, and which a change-driven client would act on.

57h. Rules made durable, because notes were not enough#

.claude/skills/spikespeak gained the two suites and the push gate, the python -u / never---hold rule (buffered stdout plus a killed process lost a whole ~20-minute suite result, and "it finished" is not "it passed"), the prove-a-test-can-fail requirement, where the vendor datasheets live, and the WebSocket command surface. It also gained a flat rule that multi-line edits go through Write/Edit, never a shell heredoc — violated three times in one night, the third while writing the rule itself, because the rule's text contained a Windows path.

Green at the end: 19/19 C++ suites, 10/10 GUI tests.

59. BOTH SUITES GREEN ON LINUX — AND THE CRASH ONLY A SECOND SESSION COULD FIND (2026-08-26)#

Configuration 1 of #52 — Linux, no hardware — run to conclusion. 20/20 C++ suites (384 cases) and 10/10 GUI tests, from a clean clone, with no path edits. The suites had never been attempted on Linux before today: both postdate the last Linux build (2026-07-06) by six weeks, so "both suites pass on Linux" had been neither true nor false, but unasked.

The build was the easy half and the tests were the hard half, which is the opposite of what the runbook budgeted for.

59a. The machine, recorded properly this time#

The July box was written up only as prose and that machine is gone. This one:
OS Linux Mint 22.1 (Xia), Ubuntu 24.04 base, kernel 6.8.0-134
CPU AMD Ryzen Threadripper 1950X, 16C/32T — AVX2 + FMA present, no AVX-512
GPU NVIDIA RTX 2080, driver 535.309.01, OpenGL 4.6 — real WebGL, no SwiftShader
Compiler GCC 13.3.0
CMake / Ninja 3.30.5 / 1.12.1, both from ~/Qt/Tools (neither is on PATH)
Qt 6.11.1, Qt online installer, ~/Qt/6.11.1/gcc_64 — the distro ships no Qt6 at all here
HDF5 1.10.10, distro libhdf5-dev, /usr/lib/x86_64-linux-gnu/hdf5/serial, CXX + HL both found
Display X11, DISPLAY=:0, two landscape monitors (1920×1080 and 2560×1440). No portrait panel.
Browser Brave (/usr/bin/brave-browser), discovered by the harness, not configured

The configure line, verbatim:

cmake -S . -B build -G Ninja -DCMAKE_BUILD_TYPE=Release -DCMAKE_PREFIX_PATH=$HOME/Qt/6.11.1/gcc_64

find_package(HDF5) needed no help — worth recording, because it was flagged as a genuine unknown on Debian-family distros. FindHDF5 prints "HDF5 CXX compiler wrapper is unable to compile a minimal HDF5 program" and then succeeds via direct library search; that line is noise. Found ALSA is the one to check for, because PortAudio's ALSA host API is silently optional and its absence surfaces much later as a red audio_panel.

59b. Fifty-three commits met GCC for the first time and nothing broke#

Zero compile errors. Zero warnings in our own code (two, both in vendored PortAudio's ALSA backend, for a deprecated snd_pcm_sw_params_set_xfer_align). The runbook explicitly budgeted "a round of trivial-but-numerous compile errors" from 53 commits of MSVC-only compilation, and the classic MSVC→GCC failures — missing transitive #includes, dependent-name lookup in templates — did not appear at all. The platform split has been maintained honestly by people who could not test it.

The one certain breaker, -mavx2 -mfma missing from the test targets, had already been fixed on 2026-08-25 and needed only confirming. Neuropixels hardware not enabled - only sim interface appeared at configure, which is the line that proves the Linux branch was entered.

A clean clone (git clone, empty ext/, PortAudio and rapidyaml FetchContent-cloned over the network at configure time) configured and built and went 20/20 with no path edits. That is #52's first criterion, closed for Linux.

59c. The sim path streams and records, counted at the socket#

Per CLAUDE.md's sim-first rule, liveness measured as binary frames at the WebSocket, not pixels:

  • 14,134 binary frames / 573.8 MB in 101.7 s = 138.95 frames/s, 5.64 MB/s, holding 138–141 frames in every active second and dropping cleanly to zero on pause.
  • Recording throughput, computed from frame.bytes in the sidecar and confirmed by the recorder's own log: 100.3 % of nominal at t+10 s, 100.0 % at t+90 s, on all three bands (ap, lfp, adc), ADC stream enabled throughout.

That second row speaks to #63, where the second Windows machine recorded at 78 % with the ADC on and decayed to ~66 % by t+90 s. Neither effect reproduces here. This is a simulated source rather than a OneBox, so it does not close #63 — but it does say the decay is not in the platform-independent record path, which narrows it usefully.

Selecting a hardware profile on this build produces the explicit toast — "Real Neuropixels hardware (PXI/OneBox) is not available in this build…" — rather than hanging half-connected, for both NPX 1.0 (OneBox) and NPX 1.0. That behaviour can only be exercised on a sim-only build, and it has not rotted.

output.log was clean across every run but for one WARN, covered in 59f.

59d. The GUI suite: 10/10, after two harness defects and one real crash#

First full run: 9/10, with band_dependency red — and red in the least useful way, because the failure report printed a single line, [harness] reusing the backend on :1234, and nothing else.

The reporter was the first bug. run_gui_tests.py ran a failing test with capture_output=True and then reported proc.stdout only. The Python traceback goes to stderr, which nothing ever read — so a crashed backend and a failed assertion looked identical. Folding stderr into stdout (the porcelain branch already did exactly this) turned it into a readable traceback immediately.

The second was the browser lifetime, and the user saw it before I did. On Linux /usr/bin/brave-browser is a #!/bin/bash wrapper that spawns the real binary as a child, so Popen holds the wrapper's pid and terminate() reaps only the wrapper. The real browser was orphaned every time: one live browser per test, three on screen before the first run was three tests old. Fixed with start_new_session=True + os.killpg, scoped to our own group so it can never reach the operator's own Brave. Windows is untouched — chrome.exe is the real binary there.

That leak turned out to be load-bearing by accident: the orphans held WebSockets open, which suppressed the backend's 2 s "last client gone" auto-quit between tests. Fixing it changed the timing that had been masking the real defect.

59e. CommandDispatcher::executeCallbacks was erasing from the vector it was iterating#

The actual failure. band_dependency passed when run alone and failed inside the suite, which is the most misleading signal a test can give.

SpikesPeak[…]: segfault at 50 … in libQt6Core.so.6.11.1
proc.lfp[…]:   segfault … in SpikesPeak

Four core dumps, every one in SpikesPeak::BandFilterProcessor::slBpHandleCommand. The backend log ended, every time, on the same line: [ spike ] band ENABLED from the GUI.

executeCallbacks ran a range-for over mSubscriptions[commandId] and, for each subscriber whose QPointer had gone null, called removeSubscriber(...) — which erases from that same vector, and, once it empties it, calls mSubscriptions.remove(commandId) and destroys the container the loop is still walking. Undefined behaviour dressed up as careful lifetime hygiene. The three [CommandDispatcher] QObject is null lines immediately before each crash are the erases happening.

It needs a stale subscriber, so it needs a second source session in one backend process. band_dependency is the only GUI test that runs after another session has been torn down — which is why a fresh backend never showed it, and why it is the only test that ever went red.

Fixed by reaping dead subscribers in one pass before anything iterates to invoke, then iterating a copy (a same-thread callback may itself add or remove subscribers). While there, removeSubscriber's guard was constructed inside its own if (lock) { … } block, so it took the mutex and released it again at the closing brace and the erase ran unguarded — and the local shadowed the parameter it was testing. Split into a locked wrapper around an unlocked body.

This is core code shared with Windows and the hardware rig. It is a correctness fix, not a Linux special case, and the crash was always possible on Windows — but it wants re-verifying on the rig before the branch merges.

Proof it was fixed, not papered over: the exact reproduction (audio_panel then band_dependency against one backend) crashed 4/4 before and passes 4/4 after, with no new core dumps; then the full suite went 10/10.

stream_options was green throughout, so #51 has not regressed on Linux.

59f. What this run did NOT close, stated plainly#

  • Every hardware row of #52. NPX_HW_ENABLED and NIDAQ_HW_ENABLED are Windows-branch-only and the vendored libraries are Windows import libraries. PXIe, OneBox, real NI-DAQ, DAC, WavePlayer and the closed-loop latency matrix are all structurally unavailable here — not skipped, impossible.
  • Closed-loop latency. It is defined as threshold crossing → stimulus pulse observed on the NI-DAQ or the OneBox ADC. There is no instrument on this box to observe the pulse, so the number cannot be produced at all. Throughput is in 59c; latency is not, and no number should be invented for it.
  • layout_matrix's portrait half. monitors() shells out to PowerShell inside except Exception: return [], so on Linux it returns empty without raising, and both the landscape and the portrait passes ran in the same window — all 16 cells in gui_layout_matrix.json reported viewport 1920x996. The cells pass, because the assertions are written in the fold rule's own live operands. They are measuring a layout no operator uses. Reported as untested, not as passing. Note that the xrandr parser #52 asks for would not have helped this box: both its monitors are landscape. Closing it needs the parser and a portrait panel.
  • One WARN in output.log, Linux-only and cosmetic: [KbListenerThread] Qt has caught an exception thrown from an event handler. At shutdown main.cpp calls QThread::terminate() on the console-input thread; on Linux that is pthread_cancel, which glibc implements by throwing a forced-unwind exception through the thread's event loop. Qt catches it, complains, and rethrows, so the unwind completes and the process still exits cleanly. Windows' TerminateThread does not throw, which is why it has never been seen. The EOF path itself is correct — KeyboardListener returns cleanly on a closed stdin and does not spin. Filed, not fixed: the thread's event loop outlives the listener, and untangling that touches the finished → QApplication::quit() wiring.

59g. Dependency decisions left for the operator, per CLAUDE.md#

None of these were taken:

  • Dropping Qt WebEngine. Still a hard find_package requirement, still zero QWebEngine* symbols in src/. It transitively supplies Qt::Widgets, Qt::Gui and Qt::WebChannel — the last is used by WebSocketServer.cpp and is requested nowhere — so removing it means naming all three explicitly first, and it changes the Windows build.
  • Xvfb. Not needed here; a real X session was available and is the evidenced route. The Xvfb path remains untested on any machine.
  • An xrandr monitor probe. Stdlib subprocess, no new dependency — but see 59f: it does not close the portrait gap on its own.
  • libnidaqmx.so. Only relevant if anyone ever wants real DAQ on Linux. Nobody has asked.

59h. Files changed#

File Change
src/core/CommandDispatch.hpp The crash fix (59e) — reap-then-copy in executeCallbacks, and a mutex guard that actually guards in removeSubscriber. Adds <algorithm>, which was arriving transitively.
tests/gui/_harness.py Browser started in its own process group and killed as a group, so Linux wrapper scripts do not orphan the real browser.
scripts/run_gui_tests.py A failing test's stderr is folded into stdout so the traceback survives to the report.

All three are portability/correctness fixes rather than Linux special cases. developer_docs/Testing.md gained a Linux section (it previously contained no occurrence of the word) and its suite table, five suites and 68 cases out of date, now reads 20/384. developer_docs/AgentTraps.md gained §5g.

Green at the end: 20/20 C++ suites (384 cases), 10/10 GUI tests, on Linux.

60. THE THROUGHPUT SHORTFALL THAT ISN'T, AND A PROBE THAT NAMES ITSELF (2026-08-26)#

Two pieces of work on the original Windows rig (OneBox 25420802, NP2014 probe): settling issue #63's throughput question with a measurement, and then the source-selection refactor — twelve hardware profiles down to three, with the probe discovered rather than declared.

60a. #63: the streams are provably unbroken, which is more than "the count was right"#

#63 reported two independent effects from the second machine: the ADC stream costing ~22 % of probe throughput, and an unrelated decay to ~66 % of nominal by t+90 s present even with the ADC disabled.

That question was already closed before this run, and I initially wrote this up as though it were not. §56 measured 4/4 cells at 100.0 % here on 2026-08-25, and the second machine then re-ran the runbook and reported 100.0 % on its own cells, including the old-vs-new-code arm on the same box that exonerates the code. This run is a third confirmation on the rig that had already confirmed it. The issue comment framing it as settling the machine-specific question has been corrected in the thread.

What it adds is the continuity check, which none of the earlier runs did. Neither reproduces: Two continuous 130 s recordings — NPX 2.0 Multishank (OneBox) and the no-ADC control — with throughput computed in 10 s windows across each run, so a decay would appear as a trend inside one file rather than as two disconnected numbers:

run band records device clock actual nominal %
with ADC raw 3,899,980 130.00 s 30,001 Hz 30,000 100.0 %
with ADC adc 3,939,290 130.00 s 30,303 Hz 30,300 100.0 %
no ADC raw 3,899,996 130.00 s 30,001 Hz 30,000 100.0 %

Every window of all three files reads 100.0 %, t+0 through t+110. The ADC costs 16 records in 3.9 million.

Counted against the device's own clock, not wall clock: the uint32 in each NPX frame is a 100 kHz tick counter, not a sample index (first row of AgentTraps.md), so (last − first) / 100000 is the device saying how long it streamed. That is what #63's step 2 asks for — measure the device, not the script's record/stop round trip.

The decisive check was the tick counter's continuity, not the rate. A low record count can mean dropped samples or a record window shorter than the script believed, and a rate cannot separate them — but a dropped sample leaves a gap. Across all 3.9 M records of each file the step alternates 3/4 ticks (averaging the expected 3.333) and the largest single step anywhere in any file is 4 ticks, 40 µs. Not one gap. So this is not "the right number of records arrived"; it is "the stream is unbroken from first sample to last".

Every earlier measurement, on both machines, counted records and divided — which cannot separate dropped samples from a record window shorter than the script believed. Continuity can, and the 12 retained second-machine recording directories can be checked for gaps with no rig time at all. That would establish whether the original shortfall was lost samples or a measurement artifact, which is the one question the re-runs cannot answer, because they no longer reproduce it.

#63 stays open, already narrowed (by the retitling comment) to the half that survives: nothing warns when a band records short, and a steady one-third loss looks exactly like a healthy rig. Today sharpens the definition of "short". The suggested stop-time check — records vs elapsed x nominal, once per band — catches a deficit spread too thinly to leave a visible gap; a continuity check catches a discrete loss and says when. The counter is monotonic with a known step, so that is a comparison of each batch's first timestamp against the previous batch's last: constant work per batch, no clock read, no allocation, nothing per-sample. The two compose.

60b. Twelve hardware profiles become three#

The Source dropdown listed twelve, because every probe/basestation pairing had been materialised as its own YAML. It now lists OneBox, PXIe, PXIe + NI-DAQ, and the simulated picker.

The reason a profile could be wrong for the probe is that a 1.0 streams hardware ap + lfp and a 2.0 streams one raw band — so a profile naming bands was a profile pinned to one probe. And it failed silently: every band name resolves through getDataByName(), which creates what you ask it to look up, so the wrong profile manufactured empty buffers, drew 384 flat traces, and wrote a real .bin and sidecar containing nothing.

The probe already names itself (np_getProbeHardwareID during setupSources), and setupSources completes before setupProcessors — so by the time any processor is configured, the registry holds exactly this probe's bands. Three of the six probe-dependent decisions already keyed off that (derived bands, ripple detector input, audio). This work added the other two and collapsed the profiles the rest had made redundant:

  • Display: inputs: is now optional; ProcessorManager binds every band hasData() reports.
  • Recorder: a recordings: entry whose band is absent is skipped with an INFO line, instead of writing a zero-length file for it.
  • Probe-mismatch warning: a profile declaring both generations is now understood as deliberately probe-agnostic and is not warned at. This also fixes the .raw-precedence flaw ConfigReference.md had recorded, where such a profile was always classified NP2.

Verified in sim on both generations, because sim covers what this rig cannot: NP1 auto-bound ap + lfp and nothing else; NP2 auto-bound raw + the two derived bands and nothing else; frames flowing in both. The check was then made to fail on purpose by naming a 1.0 band in the 2.0 profile — and the instructive part is that the phantom band bound successfully and frames kept flowing, which is precisely the silent failure being removed.

The twelve superseded profiles are gui_hidden, not deleted. Their names are how published measurements — the closed-loop latency figures among them — are reproduced, and a config's identity is its name: field. The GUI gets short; the archive stays runnable.

60c. The simulated picker cascades instead of listing sixteen#

"Simulated Data…" now asks basestation → probe → scenario. Sixteen entries in one flat list had stopped being a menu, and three of them carried the identical label "Neuropixels 1.0 — Ripple Detection (GUI)" — three buttons with the same words on them.

Both axes come from the config (sim_basestation:, sim_probe:), never from a table in the frontend, so adding a scenario stays a one-file change. An omitted axis means "independent of it" and the variant is offered under every value: the plain sine-wave sources open no basestation at all, so filing them under one would invent a distinction the data does not have. That rule is the one most likely to rot — the obvious exact-match implementation drops those variants while the picker still looks reasonable — so tests/gui/sim_picker.py asserts it from both directions, and was proven to go red when the wildcard is removed.

60c-ii. The Source control stopped being a native <select>#

Two rounds. First the picker was restyled from hand-rolled .btn-secondary rows to the app's own vocabulary — every value taken from #band-menu and #aud-dock / #aud-devmenu, the two surfaces an operator actually meets, which already agree with each other on purpose. Then the Source control itself followed: it was the last native <select> in the toolbar, and AudioModule had already written down why that is wrong — a native select's popup is OS-drawn, so it opens light-on-white in the middle of a dark acquisition GUI.

It is now #src-btn + an anchored #src-menu, and the simulated branch drills down in place rather than opening a centred modal: a dropdown that hands you to a modal halfway through a decision is two surfaces for one choice.

#selectSource is hidden, not deleted, and is still the single state holder. The menu writes its .value and dispatches the same change event a native select fired, so reflectSource()'s no-re-send logic, rebuildSourceOptions(), the control CLI, gui_scenarios.py and the GUI tests all work unchanged. syncSourceButton() mirrors label and disabled state from it, on every path including the remote ones.

Three defects, all of which looked cosmetic and were not.

  1. The picker's backdrop was z-index: 1000 — below #aud-dock and #band-menu at 2147480000. An open dock covered the modal. Now 2147480002, still below #cfm-veil and #hwfault-veil so a confirmation or hardware fault opens over it.
  2. The menu's stylesheet was injected lazily on first open, and it carries the rule that hides #selectSource — so the native select sat visible in the toolbar beside its own replacement until somebody opened the menu once. Injected at startup now. The test caught this, by asserting the select is genuinely out of sight rather than assuming the CSS had applied.
  3. gui_hidden filtered only the hardware dropdown, so a simulated profile marked hidden was still offered in the picker — the flag half-worked, silently. It now filters both, which also removes the last duplicate-label case (the two latency benches carry the same sim_variant label as the ordinary ripple scenario).

tests/gui/sim_picker.py was rewritten against the menu and now covers seven invariants, including that the visible trigger cannot disagree with the hidden state element. It also stopped selecting the play-file box by its literal inline background colour — a selector no restyle could have survived.

60c-iii. The picker's earlier modal form#

Its first version used .btn-secondary rows on a bare card. That is a third dialect: the two surfaces an operator actually meets — the Bands menu and the audio dock — already agree with each other on purpose, and the picker matched neither. Restyled so every value comes from #band-menu and #aud-dock / #aud-devmenu: dock shell (#2a2e39 / #3a3f4b, radius 8, 12.5px), a title bar with a hairline and an h2 at 14px/600, borderless rows with the same 9px status dot and #262c36 hover, #8d97a5 sub-lines, the uppercase letter-spaced group label for the breadcrumb, and the audio dock's inset-panel treatment for the play-file form. No new colours were invented; the mapping is written above SIM_PICKER_CSS so a future change to one surface can be carried across.

One thing that looked cosmetic and was not. The picker's backdrop was z-index: 1000 — below #aud-dock and #band-menu at 2147480000. An open dock covered the modal. It is now 2147480002: above the docks and menus, still below #cfm-veil and #hwfault-veil so a confirmation or a hardware fault opens over it.

Two things the restyle also forced, both improvements: gui_hidden now filters this picker (it only ever filtered the hardware dropdown, so the flag half-worked and silently — the two latency benches are now hidden, which removes the last duplicate-label case), and tests/gui/sim_picker.py stopped selecting the play-file box by its literal inline background colour, which no restyle could have survived. It selects by class, and asserts no two choices on a leaf share a label.

60d. Two defects found along the way#

Every recording's sidecar had a NUL-padded part number. probe.part_number was written as "NP2014" followed by 58 embedded NULs: the vendor field is a fixed-width char[] and trimmed() strips whitespace, not NULs. Every match in the codebase uses startsWith(), so detection and the

65 microvolt scale were never wrong — but an offline tool comparing == "NP2014" fails silently.#

Cut at the first NUL now, still bounded by sizeof() first.

The GUI harness's startup diagnostics were dead on Windows. _gui_backend.log exists so a failed startup names its cause instead of collapsing into a 30-second silence; on Windows it was 261 bytes every time. The backend is built WIN32 — GUI subsystem, no stdout or stderr to inherit — so the redirect captures only the launcher's two lines and everything the app says goes to output.log. The harness now reads both. Recorded as AgentTraps.md §5h, including that output.log rotates on the next start, so a script running the backend twice must copy it or read the wrong run.
