60e. Files changed#

File Change
src/core/ProcessorManager.cpp Binds display streams from the bands the source actually produced, before the derived-band block.
src/modules/recording/RecordProcessor.cpp Skips a recordings: entry whose band no source provides.
src/modules/neuropixels/DisplayProcessor.{hpp,cpp} inputs: optional; ensureDerivedBandStream takes a channel width so it can bind the 12-wide ADC band.

This is Phase 1 / Option A of NextTask_SourceArchitecture.md (2026-07-24), reached by a different route: the brief proposed a config-layering include: loader to collapse the cross-product, and none was needed, because the axis doing most of the multiplying is discoverable. Phases 2 and 3 — a parameterized NPX sim, a file-playback source, the descriptor model — are untouched and still open.

61. THE RIG NAMES ITSELF (2026-08-26, late)#

The source-selection work finished the job it started: after the probe, the BASESTATION and the PROBE COUNT are read from the hardware too. Plus the renderer-swap groundwork.

61a. One entry, because a OneBox and a PXIe module are the same thing to the API#

np_scanBS() enumerates every basestation and np_getDeviceList() reports each one's platformid. np_openBS() is where the exclusive claim starts, and the scan never gets there -- so "which basestation is this?" is answerable for free, before the operator commits to anything.

Both source classes were already making exactly those calls, separately. They are now one cached helper (BasestationScan.hpp), and source.npx.auto resolves to source.npx.onebox or source.npx.pxi from it. The menu is Neuropixels + PXIe with NI-DAQ + Simulated Data.

Ambiguity is asked about, never resolved by a rule. Both attached => the connect refuses and the menu asks which, because only one is supported at a time. Not theoretical: this machine reports 1 PXIe module and 1 OneBox, and the prompt is what it shows.

61b. The NI-DAQ stays an explicit choice -- and the reason I first gave was wrong#

PxieNiDaq.yaml said the card "has to be reserved" and so could not be detected like a probe. That conflated two things the code keeps apart: DAQmxGetSysDevNames lists devices with no claim, and the reservation is not taken until DAQmx_Val_Task_Commit at connect. It also drew a false parallel -- the probe's basestation is claimed exclusively too. Retracted in the file.

The real reason it stays explicit: whether the DAQ was captured is a provenance fact about the recording, and its absence is only discoverable AFTER the experiment. No _nidaq_ file means no ai4 edge, which means closed-loop latency cannot be computed at all. Auto-using a card "if one is there" turns a loud pre-experiment failure into a quiet post-experiment discovery.

Detection is still used: the NI-DAQ entry is hidden on a rig with no PXIe module, via a new probeHardware command whose answer the backend caches.

61c. More than one probe is a question#

detectProbePort() returns the FIRST port with a headstage -- arbitrary, on a rig with several. A new listProbePorts() counts them, and more than one stops the connect and asks which port to use. The choice lives in a static, because the question is raised by a connect that FAILS and has to outlive it. The dialog says plainly that multi-probe acquisition is in the backlog and not a priority, so nobody has to wonder whether the restriction is a bug.

listProbePorts() is separate from detectProbePort() rather than a mode on it: the latter carries the OneBox's 8-attempt power-up retry, and rewriting two hardware paths whose retry behaviour cannot be exercised without the hardware is not worth doing for tidiness.

61d. The Source control stopped being a native <select>#

It was the last one in the toolbar. AudioModule had already made this call for its device picker and written down why: a native select's popup is OS-drawn, so it opens light-on-white in the middle of a dark GUI. It is now #src-btn + an anchored #src-menu in the app's own palette, with the simulated cascade drilling down IN PLACE rather than jumping to a modal.

#selectSource is hidden, not deleted, and remains the single state holder -- the menu writes its .value and fires the same change event, so reflectSource(), the CLI and the GUI tests are untouched.

Three defects, all of which looked cosmetic and were not: the picker sat at z-index: 1000, BELOW the audio dock at 2147480000 (an open dock covered it); the menu's stylesheet was injected lazily on first open and carries the rule that hides #selectSource, so the native select sat visible beside its own replacement until someone opened the menu once; and gui_hidden filtered only the hardware dropdown, so a hidden simulated profile was still offered in the picker.

61e. Tests#

tests/test_basestationscan.cpp -- 15 cases, linking the REAL BasestationScan.cpp. The important one asserts the REFUSAL: both transports attached must not resolve to a source name. It also pins that "unambiguous" is about the KIND of basestation (two OneBoxes is still one transport) and that apiAvailable == false is distinguishable from "nothing attached" -- one is an operator problem, the other is a build that cannot look.

tests/gui/sim_picker.py rewritten against the menu: seven invariants including that the visible trigger cannot disagree with the hidden state element, and that a hidden profile is hidden in both pickers. It stopped selecting the play-file box by its literal inline background colour -- a selector no restyle could survive, and this restyle proved it.

22 C++ suites, 11 GUI tests.

61f. Groundwork for the renderer swap#

developer_docs/RendererContract.md -- 524 lines, the normative spec any SciChart replacement must satisfy: the interface, the data contract, the visual contract (literal colours and spacing, because the look must not change), the benchmark protocol, and the traps.

benchmarks/cdp_bench.py was silently broken and is fixed. It connected the sim by clicking a button inside #sim-picker-card -- an element that stopped existing when the picker became an anchored menu -- and the click was guarded with if(b)b.click(), so it did nothing, raised nothing, and the sweep measured an IDLE, UNCONNECTED view at a beautiful 60 fps in every cell. It now drives the backend over the WebSocket like every GUI test. Also fixed: the source name matched two configs once the OneBox sim landed; #channelsPerStrip silently overrode the sweep variable and persisted in localStorage; buildColumns() was not awaited; and the output path was hard-coded so a second run overwrote the first. window.__benchPointCount() -- documented in benchmarks/README.md and never implemented -- now exists.

The frozen baseline CSV is therefore a historical reference, not a control arm. SciChart must be re-measured on today's tree with the corrected harness before any replacement is compared to it.

61g. Archived#

Six briefs whose work is done: NextTask_OneBoxSupport, NextSession_SecondMachine_OneBox, NextTask_WindowsReverify, NextTask_ThroughputOnSecondMachine, NextTask_LinuxBringUp, NextTask_SyncPulseCountCheck -- each with a row in developer_docs/archive/README.md saying what superseded it. Links repointed in both directions; docs book rebuilds with 0 broken links.

62. THE CHART LIBRARY IS GONE (2026-08-27)#

SciChart.js v5.2.35 is removed from SpikesPeak. The live traces are drawn by a hand-written WebGL2 renderer and the probe map by plain canvas 2D. webinterface/lib/ went from 3.5 MB to 16 kB — one file, qwebchannel.js.

This closes the rule in CLAUDE.md that had said, since 2026-08-24, that the GUI was "on notice".

62a. What replaced what#

was is file
FastLineRenderableSeries x N per column one vertex buffer, one drawArrays per column, primitive restart between traces webinterface/GlRenderer.js
XyScatterRenderableSeries x 7 + box/custom/text annotations + 2 chart modifiers canvas 2D: circles, rectangles, triangles, text webinterface/ProbeMap.js
two XyDataSeries FIFOs per channel, in DataStore nothing — the renderer holds the only copy of the samples webinterface/DataStore.js
SciChartSurface.create() per column new GlColumnRenderer(chartAreaDiv) webinterface/ChartColumn.js
initImroSciChart() initProbeMap() webinterface/ImroManager.js

The probe map was the larger half of the work, not the live view. The live view had already been proven on the WebGL renderer; the map was still a full scatter chart with drag-selection, wheel-zoom and per-point text labels. What made it tractable was that renderElectrodeMap() and renderRecordSelection() — about 700 lines of electrode, bank and shank logic — only ever called clear() and appendRange(xs, ys, metas) on their series. ProbeMap keeps exactly that API, so none of that logic was touched. A rewrite that had to re-derive which electrode maps to which channel would have been a different and much worse proposition, and the shim was worth the slightly old-fashioned API to avoid it.

62b. What it cost, measured on the rig#

OneBox + Neuropixels 2.0, four columns of 120 = 480 on-screen traces, n=4 interleaved repeats:

SciChart WebGL2
renderer main thread 0.964 ± 0.004 0.225 ± 0.006 4.28x less
all browser processes 1.327 ± 0.030 cores 0.541 ± 0.052 2.45x less
GPU utilization 38.0 ± 0.4 % 35.9 ± 0.2 % 1.06x — parity
GPU power 19.8 W 19.6 W 1.01x — parity
frame rate 42 ± 5 fps 60 ± 0 —

The GPU is at parity and that is the point. The saving is CPU, and it is not displaced onto the GPU. The old library kept a live object per trace and walked all 480 every frame — scene graph, per-series coordinate transform, JS/WASM crossings. That per-series bookkeeping was the cost. The replacement has no per-series objects, so the per-frame work is O(1) draw calls with the arithmetic on hardware that was already idle. Full write-up, including the claim that did NOT survive contact with hardware, in developer_docs/RendererExperiment.md.

62c. Four defects the removal exposed#

Each of these was live before the removal, or would have shipped with it.

  1. The channel labels were pinned to the wrong box. The label overlay was positioned from the library's seriesViewRect, which the (invisible, label-less) x-axis inset by exactly 5 px at the bottom — while the WebGL canvas is inset:0 and drew into the full div. So under WebGL every label sat progressively high of its trace: 13 % of a row at 20 rows, about 80 % of a row at 120. With the library gone the plot area is the div and they align by construction.

  2. A deliberate teardown reported itself as a fault. GlColumnRenderer.destroy() calls loseContext(), which fires webglcontextlost synchronously — so the new "context lost" handler fired on every column rebuild (orientation flip, column count, density change) and told the operator the live view had broken. The listener now ignores a loss it caused.

  3. Two guards became permanently false, silently. handleNewStream() and handleWindowResize() both tested columns[0].wasmContext — the library's handle, used as a proxy for "the view is ready". Removing the library made both tests always-false with no error anywhere: no stream was ever registered and the live view sat on "Waiting for data..." forever. This is the failure mode this repo keeps meeting — a proxy for a condition, rather than the condition. The guards now test what they mean (columns.length > 0).

  4. The plot background lived only inside the bundle. The radial gradient behind every column came from SciChartSurfaceBase.DEFAULT_THEME and appeared in no stylesheet, so deleting the bundle deleted it. It is now explicit in style.css on .chart-container-wrapper, and the trace canvas clears to transparent so it shows through. developer_docs/RendererContract.md had recorded the exact values, which is the only reason it was recoverable.

A fifth, caught by review before it shipped: the chart div was a containing block only because the library set position:relative on it inline. The absolutely-positioned trace canvas then resolved against the wrapper instead — the same box today, by coincidence of the current layout. Now stated in CSS.

62d. Testing#

The suite no longer selects a renderer: SPIKESPEAK_GUI_RENDERER, window.__setGl and window.__useGl are gone. Instead _harness.py asserts that a column has a live renderer and refuses to run otherwise — because an empty canvas and a rig sending nothing look identical, and the suite has already been caught passing vacuously once (2026-08-25, c.sciChartSurface). The draws-anything probe in live_view.py now asks the renderer for its allocated row count.

Before the removal, the full GUI suite was run green under both renderers — 11/11 on SciChart and 11/11 on WebGL2 — which is the evidence that the swap changed nothing an operator can see. Four tests that previously left no visual evidence (sim_picker, shrink_window, record_flow, error_paths) now each save a screenshot, so every test in the suite is inspectable.

63. WHAT A CLICK ACTUALLY SELECTS (2026-08-27, later)#

Three changes, all about the probe map being trustworthy rather than merely working.

63a. The probe map is now tested where it matters#

62 added a test that the map DRAWS. That is not the question an operator cares about. The question is whether a click lands on the electrode they aimed at — and getting that wrong is invisible: the map looks correct, the recording is of different sites, and you find out from the data.

The test now computes the target pixel itself from the published axis ranges (window.probeMapTest.geometry()), drives a real mouse event through CDP, and asserts on the resulting IMRO channel map. Computing the pixel independently is the whole point: a test that reused ProbeMap's own transform could only prove it was self-consistent with itself.

Proven able to fail, which is the only reason to keep it. Injecting a one-row error into pxToY trips six checks with the exact signature you would want:

NP1.0 click: aiming at electrode 630 (row 315, col 0) must route channel 246 to bank 1; it is on bank 0
NP1.0 click on electrode 630: exactly channel 246 may change; channels [248] changed
NP1.0 drag: dragging rows 286..344 must select rows 286..477; it selected 287..478

Two things had to be fixed in the test before it could fail at all, and both are worth recording:

  • The first version could not fail on NP1. A fresh map routes every channel to bank 0 and the view opens on that block, so every electrode on screen was already selected — a click asked the app to do what it had already done, and "did the bank change?" was vacuously true wherever the click landed. NP2 caught the injected error; NP1 sailed through. The test now scrolls into an unselected bank first, and asserts that it managed to, rather than passing on a no-op.
  • Channel 191 is skipped by a drag, because its electrodes are internal-reference sites. The first drag assertion counted it and reported the selection as spanning the whole probe. The app was right; the check was wrong.

63b. NP1.0 selection is free-form#

The operator asked for arbitrary per-channel selection on 1.0 probes, noting we appeared to offer only banks. Per-channel selection already worked — clicking electrode E routes channel E mod 384 to bank E div 384 on its own, NpInterface.cpp validates each (channel, bank) pair independently, and np_selectElectrode is called per channel. The new test proves it end to end.

What was bank-like was the drag: it padded the dragged span out to exactly 192 rows so the result always covered all 384 channels exactly once. Tidy, and always valid — but not the hardware's rule, and it meant a 50-row drag silently produced a 192-row selection. Electrodes nobody asked for.

The drag now assigns exactly what it covers, matching NP2.0's drag, OpenEphys and SpikeGLX. Channels outside the run keep the electrodes they had, which is always legal because each channel picks its own bank independently. The Bank buttons still fill all 384 channels at one depth in a click, so nothing that relied on a complete window is lost.

The test asserts the outside-is-untouched property directly, since that is the substance of the change: reinstating the snap moves 262 channels that were never dragged over, and the check says so by name.

63c. T-13: stale drag affordance on reused labels#

Channel labels are reused across draws — only their text is rewritten. label.onmousedown and the grab cursor were set only in the probe-electrode branch and never cleared, so a label that had once shown an electrode kept the Ctrl+drag-to-ripple gesture after the column switched to the OneBox ADC or NI-DAQ stream: it still looked draggable, and dragging it fired with an electrode index belonging to a stream no longer on screen.

Cleared unconditionally at the top of the per-slot iteration rather than in each branch that does not want it — so a branch added later cannot inherit the same bug. Without the fix the new assertion reports 20 labels still carrying a handler and a grab cursor.

64. THE MAP THAT DREW PERFECTLY AND DID NOTHING (2026-08-27, evening)#

Found by the operator on the rig, in the least alarming possible form: "I can't scroll up and down on the probe map view."

64a. Probe identity was a string match against the prototype part id#

probeIdStr is a part-TYPE id. The simulator reports the PROTOTYPE ("24" for a 4-shank 2.0); real hardware reports the true part, and the shipping 4-shank part is NP2013 -> "2013". Seven places in ImroManager.js identified the probe by matching that string against "24", "21", "NP2...".

On the rig every one of them answered not a Neuropixels probe:

what broke how it looked
wheel handler early-returns cannot scroll the probe map — the reported symptom
mousedown / mouseup guards clicks and drags do nothing
lvKind never set the live view never builds its depth ordering
updateQuickFormatVisibility Shank / Bank preset buttons hidden
appliedRecordInfo, renderRecordSelection record selection treats a 2.0 as a 1.0
probeFriendlyName probe named (2013)

Rendering is not gated on probe type, so the map drew perfectly throughout. That is why this reads as a scrolling complaint rather than a dead editor, and why nobody caught it sooner.

The suite could not have caught it: sim spells the id the way the old code expected. So the fix is a single probeGen() helper keying off probeType — the ProbeType enum the C++ branches on and has been sending in getImroAsJson all along — and the test pins the RULE on synthetic payloads including {probeType: 3, probeIdStr: "2013"}. Reverting probeGen to string matching turns that test red on exactly the two real-hardware cases. Written up as AgentTraps.md 5i.

64b. The auto-detect refused correctly and told the operator the wrong thing#

Connecting the auto-detecting Neuropixels profile on a rig with both a OneBox and a PXIe module reported:

The selected source module "source.npx.auto" is not available in this build.

Two independent faults produced that sentence:

  1. WebSocketServer looked the RAW config name up in the source registry. source.npx.auto is not a registered module — it is a request to read the rig — so the lookup failed and fell into the "not compiled into this build" branch. SourceManager resolved it properly; the two never compared notes.
  2. resolveAutoSource() writes a precise, operator-facing reason for every refusal — "A OneBox and a PXIe basestation are both attached. Only one can be used at a time" — and sent it to qWarning(). The log had the right answer the whole time.

So the operator was sent to look for a broken build when the actual situation was two basestations and a question. WebSocketServer now resolves the auto name from the same cached scan, and relays the real reason.

And the ambiguous case is now a choice, not an error. Nothing is broken when two basestations are attached — the rig has more hardware than SpikesPeak streams, and which one this recording is on is the one fact auto-detection cannot read off the hardware. It opens a dialog offering OneBox or PXIe, exactly as a multi-probe rig is offered its ports. error_paths.py asserts it opens, has real size (the 0x0-invisible-card trap has bitten one of these dialogs before), offers both buttons, and is not the blocking hardware-error dialog.

64c. Two test-authoring notes worth keeping#

  • A inside a non-raw Python triple-quoted string becomes a REAL newline before it reaches the browser, which is a syntax error inside a JS string literal. The eval then does nothing, silently, and the test fails blaming the feature. Use an r-string for any JS payload containing escapes.
  • build.bat pauses by default and will hang a scripted chain at "Press any key to continue". --no-pause exists for exactly this, and the file says so.

65. THE MORNING OF AN IN-VIVO SESSION (2026-08-28)#

An animal was going in today. Everything below was found in the hours before it, most of it by asking the rig rather than the code, and every hardware-facing fix was verified on the rig.

65a. A pre-experiment hardware smoke, and what it was for#

The probe-identity fix from 64a had never met real hardware — sim reports part id "24" and the shipping probe reports its own, so no simulated run could confirm it. A scripted smoke did:

probeKind='NP2'  probeIdStr='2014'

2014, not the 2013 I had assumed. String matching would have failed on it whichever literal I had added; keying off the ProbeType enum works regardless, which is the point of 64a.

19/19 checks: probe named properly, Shank/Bank buttons visible, wheel scrolls, a click routes exactly one channel, Apply Map to Hardware succeeds, streaming with depth ordering (labels 19R 18L 17R 16L), 20 s recorded (510 MB raw + 20 MB adc with sidecars), clean teardown.

Two of the early "failures" were the test, not the app: the record dialog only EXISTS after Record is pressed while idle, and build.bat pauses by default and will hang a scripted chain at "Press any key to continue" (--no-pause exists for exactly this).

65b. The sidecar and the live view disagreed about microvolts#

NpInterface.cpp:1507 has resolved the probe's part number through Npx2Scale.hpp since #65 and hands the result to the display — so what an operator SEES has been right. The block that writes the recording sidecar was never wired to the same table and hardcoded

b.voltsPerCount = 1.0 / 84.0 / 4096.0;   // gain 84 belongs to NO NP2.0 variant

Prototypes are gain 80, commercial parts gain 100. On the NP2014 that wrote 2.906 µV/count where 2.441 is true — 1.190× — while the screen showed 2.441. One recording described by two different scales is worse than a uniform error: anyone reconciling an export against the display finds a discrepancy with no visible cause. Now resolved from the part number, provenance device-readback, and an unrecognised part writes an unavailable_reason into the file instead of scaling by a guess.

65c. A GUI-started recording wrote no ripple sidecar#

slWriteRippleSidecar had exactly one caller: the WebSocket setRecording handler. The GUI Record button invokes the recorder directly over QWebChannel and never reaches it — so every recording an operator started by pressing Record, which is every in-vivo session, was written without its detection electrodes or its trained per-channel baselines.

Counts can be rescaled afterwards. A trained baseline cannot be reconstructed from anything on disk, so the detections in such a recording could not be explained after the fact.

The trigger moved to RecordProcessor::setRecording, the one point both paths pass through. The new test drives the button, because driving the WebSocket path passes even with the bug present — which is exactly how it survived.

65d. The tab bar highlighted a panel that was not showing#

Reported from the rig: connected, landed on Live Data with Probe Map still highlighted. Two faults in one branch:

if (sourceName.includes("NPX")) { ...show map, set active... }
else { tabs.hide(); viewMap.hide(); viewData.show(); }   // never moves 'active'

The else moved the panel and left the highlight. And includes("NPX") is false for every real rig — the hardware profiles are OneBox / PXIe / Neuropixels since the source-selection refactor, while every SIMULATED profile still says NPX. The correct branch ran in simulation and the broken one on hardware, which is why the suite never saw it. Third instance of the same mistake in two days: asking a display string a question about hardware.

selectMainTab() now owns both halves. A third instance of the same gate in NetworkClient.js had inverted into "reset stale probe state on hardware, keep it in simulation" — now unconditional.

65e. The exports described the wrong probe#

Asked whether everything recorded can be converted for post-hoc analysis. Yes for today's bands — but verifying it found two defects that would have corrupted an export silently.

SpikeGLX carried only the PROTOTYPE four-shank spec (PRB2_4_2_0640_0, type 24, 14-bit, gain 80). SpikeGLX reconstructs volts as Vmax/Imax/gain, so a commercial NP2014 exported as 0.5/8192/80 = 0.7629 µV/count against the true 0.5/2048/100 = 2.4414 — a 3.2× error.

And geometry was fabricated: geom_entry() hardcoded shank = 0 for every channel, with a comment saying a real multi-shank map "would come from the probe — which SpikesPeak does not record". It does: columns.shank carries 384 values and acquisition.imro.text is the applied map verbatim, already in SpikeGLX's own format. On a four-shank probe the export claimed every site was on shank 0, and geometry is precisely what a sorter consumes.

Both now come from the recording. Verified on a real NP2014: imDatPrb_pn NP2014, type 2014, imMaxInt 2048, gain 100 → 2.4414 µV/count, ~imroTbl verbatim, ~snsGeomMap shanks [0, 1]. Open Ephys gained shank / xpos / ypos, which it had none of.

Recorded because it is a property of the recording, not the tools: both converters refuse the NP1.0 AP band. Its per-frame timestamp is not a per-sample clock — inside each 12-sample IMEC packet the stamps advance ≈4.36 ticks/sample then step back by 8 — so ~8 % of frames do not advance, and forcing them inflates the axis to 1.3057× and invents ~110 000 gaps. Refusing beats writing a plausible lie. Convert the LFP band.

python/README.md had documented a "3.81× disagreement between the two converters" and left it to the operator. Neither of its two numbers was right — one was the no-such-variant gain 84, the other the prototype spec.

First conversion documentation: user_docs/Exporting-Recordings.md and developer_docs/DataConversion.md.

65f. #50 and #59 closed#

#50 — the comparator thresholds had been implemented and confirmed on the rig since 08-26 and nothing had ever called them. The OneBox dock now has the panel (device read-back, not what was typed — the device quantizes; Reset-all, because thresholds persist across sessions). One scope bullet was closed as unbuildable: there is no np_ADC_enableChannel in the vendor API, only ADC_enableProbe all-twelve-or-none.

#59 — closed as recovered and mitigated, explicitly not as fixed. Root cause never found; the box recovered on a power-cycle and has not failed since. What makes it safe to close is that the dangerous property is gone: np_readPackets returning SUCCESS while delivering nothing is now surfaced by slSourceStalled as an operator-facing error, so a recurrence is visible while it happens rather than in the data afterwards.

65g. Two things an operator has to know#

Both of these were fixed on 2026-08-28 — see §66. They are left here as written because this log is dated: the entries below describe what was true when the OneBox matrix was run, and any recording made before that date has the layout they describe.

  • <name>_ripple.json is written BESIDE the recording folder, not inside it. Archive the parent session directory or a copy leaves it behind.
  • Per-event detections and stim times are never written to disk — they exist only as live WebSocket messages. Capture the stream during a closed-loop run or they are gone.

66. A RECORDING NOW CARRIES ITS OWN PROVENANCE (2026-08-28)#

The two operator-facing gaps §65g had to warn about, closed. Both are about the same thing: a closed- loop recording should be able to explain itself months later, from the folder alone.

66a. The sidecar was written one directory too high#

RecordProcessor nests every band into <dir>/<name>/. RippleDetector::writeRippleSidecar wrote to <dir>/<name>_ripple.json — beside the folder, not in it. Two consequences, one of which had already cost a re-run:

  • Copying or archiving "the recording" left the detection channels and trained thresholds behind.
  • analyze_recording.py's auto-discovery could never find it. Its recdir argument is the folder holding the .bin.jsons, so the glob looked inside while the file sat outside, and --ripple-sidecar had to be passed by hand on every cell of the OneBox latency matrix. The instruction to pass it is still in NextTask_OneBoxLatencyMatrix.md, now marked as no longer needed rather than deleted.

The sidecar is written inside the folder as of today. The analysis tools read both layouts and print which one they found — every recording made before today has the old one, and those are precisely the recordings being re-analysed.

66b. Nothing on disk said what the loop actually did#

rippleDetected, rippleLatency and rippleStimFired were emitted only as live WebSocket messages. Unless somebody was capturing the socket at the time, a finished closed-loop session could not say when it fired or how long it took — and no export format carried it either. The sidecar records the configuration; nothing recorded the session.

Those three events now append to <dir>/<name>/<name>_events.jsonl, one JSON object per line, flushed as written so a session that ends badly still has everything up to its last complete line. The file opens lazily on the first event and closes on record stop.

rippleEnvelope is deliberately excluded, and the reason is the rule in CLAUDE.md twice over: it streams at ~25 Hz, so writing it would put a file append on the detection path, and it is fully recomputable from the recorded band, so it would be recording what can be recomputed. The three that are kept are the ones that cannot be: a detection is a decision the online quorum made against a trained baseline, and a stim time is an event in the world.

66c. The test that could not see either bug#

tests/gui/ripple_sidecar.py globbed outdir/** recursively, so it passed whether the sidecar was inside the folder or above it. It now asserts the sidecar's directory equals the bands' directory.

Making the event assertions mean anything took more than adding them. The test sets z = 0 — "fire on anything above the mean", which RippleModule supports deliberately for exactly this — and waits for a detection to reach the page before starting the recording, so an empty events file can only mean the writer is broken, never that the window happened to be quiet.

Two things that cost time getting there, both worth keeping:

  • Adding electrodes does not start the detector. The dock's #rd-run button does, and the baseline estimation window (~13 s) only begins then. The first version of the test set z = 0 and waited 90 s on a detector reporting running: false and a null threshold the entire time.
  • Do not poll the cowsay. It is rewritten by the next event and cleared on lockout, so a poll landing in a gap sees nothing and concludes the detector is dead. Wrapping rm.onDetected gives a counter that only goes up.

Proved by injecting both regressions at once and watching exactly the two new checks go red, naming the defect in each case ("Sidecar is in '.', bands are in 'sidecar_probe'"), then reverting.

67. THE CONTROL SURFACE ANSWERS BACK (2026-08-28)#

Issues #51, #63, #64 and #61/#62, worked in one pass because three of them turn out to be the same sentence: the system told a client something that was not true, and the client could not tell.

67a. Two of the four were already half-fixed, and the issues did not know it#

Before writing anything, an audit of the implementation surface turned up commit 1a8b725 ("Report a short recording, and stop ignoring commands in silence", 2026-08-25) already on this branch. It had added the terminal else #64 asks for and the record-stop shortfall check #63 asks for. Both issues still described the code as it was before that commit, and so did developer_docs/AgentTraps.md — the file CLAUDE.md tells every agent to read second, asserting a defect that no longer existed.

Check the tree before implementing from an issue. Two of the four items here would have been written twice.

67b. #64 — a warning nobody receives is not a report#

The terminal else logged a qWarning and stopped. That reaches output.log, and the sender of an unrecognized command is by definition a script, the CLI or an agent — none of which read it. The four-cell hardware run that motivated the issue would still have failed exactly the same way.

Every other refusal on this surface already replies to the client: a refused select, a refused play and a refused setRecording all broadcast guiStatus. Log-only was the outlier. It now broadcasts.

Three more things came out of that, none of which were in the issue:

  • A known name missing its value was reported as an unrecognized NAME. That sends the author hunting for a typo that does not exist. The two cases are now distinguished.
  • Disconnect demanded a value its body throws away. So {"command":"Disconnect"} — the most likely hand-written command on this whole surface — fell through and was reported as an unknown name, when the name was exactly right. The guard is deleted.
  • RippleDetector::slApplyCommand had the same gap and a worse consequence. It echoes full state after every action, unconditionally, so an unknown action came back with a plausible-looking rippleState — a fabricated ACK rather than silence. It is also the highest-traffic scripted surface in the repo. An unknown action now warns and returns without echoing.

67c. #51 — narrowed to the transient, and the transient is gone#

The steady state was already correct, which is why two earlier investigations concluded it was fixed. What survived was one broadcast wide: stateSource went out naming the requested source before the connect was attempted, and was corrected only afterwards. A client that polls never notices. A client that reacts to a state change — starts a recording, relabels a file, rebuilds a band list — acts on a source that never connected.

The broadcast was early for an ordering reason, not a timing one: deviceReady triggers the browser's QWebChannel bind and that bind is keyed on the source name, so stateSource must arrive first. Moving it to immediately before setDeviceState preserves that invariant exactly and emits nothing at all on the refusal path. The visible cost is that the picker no longer shows the new name during a slow connect — which is the honest rendering, because during that window it is not the source.

67d. #63 — the half that was closable, and the half that is not#

Closed. The shortfall warning now travels sigIpErrorOccurred, which is relayed to the toolbar for every processor, so a band recording at 66 % of nominal is visible to somebody watching the GUI rather than only to somebody reading output.log afterwards.

Also closed, and it was worse than the issue knew. Nothing called setRecording(false) on any teardown path — teardownSource() paused the stream and deregistered the recorders, and its comment claimed recording is "stopped by pausing the stream downstream", which was never true. So a recording ended with Disconnect or quit skipped its entire stop path: no shortfall check, no ripple sidecar rewrite with the trained thresholds, no explicit flush and close of the event log, and no log line saying where the file went. The one session you most want the check for — the one that ended badly — was exactly the one that never reported.

Still open, and deliberately. #63 needs a per-batch continuity check on the 100 kHz tick counter (each batch's first timestamp against the previous batch's last: constant work per batch, no clock read, no allocation) to say when samples were lost, where the stop-time ratio only says how many. And it needs the measurement repeated on this rig to settle general-versus-machine-specific. Neither is done. The control config Npx2OneBoxNoAdc.yaml the issue asks to be committed already is — that part of the issue is stale.

67e. #61 / #62 — the term everyone expected to differ does not#

NP1.0 on PXIe, measured, filling one of the three empty cells of the eye/ear matrix:

term NP2.0, OneBox (2026-08-25) NP1.0, PXIe (2026-08-28)
source_lag_mean_ms 1.00 ms 1.00 ms
audio_ring_depth_ms 15.11 ms 3.45 ms
output_latency_measured_ms 99.90 ms 18.87 ms
raw → ear ~116.0 ms ~23.3 ms
raw → eye 19.2 ms 19.4 ms

#61 expected the source lag to differ because PXIe delivers over the PCIe backplane in a different block cadence from the OneBox's FT601 USB path. It does not — 1.00 ms, same to two decimals. #62 expected it might differ the other way, because NP1.0 hands the audio path a native AP band the probe already high-passed while NP2.0 makes the audio processor derive the band itself. Also 1.00 ms. Verified on the right band: input_band = data.npx.ap, filtered = false.

The 5x in the ear is the audio device, not the rig. Both runs took whatever device came up by default and they came up on different ones — MME/Sound Mapper at 99.90 ms measured on 2026-08-25, DirectSound/Primary Sound Driver at 18.87 ms today. AudioVideoLatency.md §2 already ranks nine devices from 8.71 to 99.90 ms on this same machine, so that spread is squarely inside what the page documents. Comparing ear totals is only meaningful with the device held fixed, and the doc now says so where the table is.

Two cells remain and each needs one physical probe move. After these two rows there is little reason to expect either to differ: the source lag has now been shown constant across a transport change and a probe generation, which are the only variables they introduce.

67f. #52 — audited against the evidence, and it cannot close#

Twenty-two agents, every "satisfied" verdict attacked from two angles. Two of the five configurations have a dated record of actually being run, not four:

# configuration verdict
1 Linux, no hardware green 2026-08-26 — but 22 commits and a whole renderer replacement ago
2 Windows, no hardware never run
3 Windows + OneBox + PXIe no suite result recorded in the both-attached state
4 Windows + PXIe, no OneBox no C++ or GUI suite counts recorded
5 Windows + OneBox, no PXIe GUI was 5/6, stream_options red — an explained red, not a pass

Configuration 1's staleness is the load-bearing one: SciChart came out and the hand-written WebGL2 renderer went in after that run, on Windows only. The Linux 10/10 was against SciChart, and two C++ suites, thirty cases and five GUI tests added since have never been compiled by GCC.

developer_docs/SecondMachineRunbooks.md is new: one runbook per configuration, with what each proves, what it cannot, the exact commands, what to paste back into the issue, and the trap that layout_matrix passes on a landscape-only machine while measuring a layout no operator uses.

scripts/requirements.txt was rewritten. The headline it was missing: both suites need nothing installed — they import only the standard library and this repo's own modules. The old flat list of numpy/scipy/h5py read as "install these before you can test", which is false and is the kind of prerequisite a newcomer gives up on.

67g. Two test-authoring notes, both paid for#

  • ensure_backend() is now separate from Gui, so a test that talks only to the WebSocket does not launch a browser it never uses. Extracting it, I inserted the module-level function inside the class body, which silently terminated the class — Gui lost __enter__ and every method after it. Caught immediately by the next run, and worth noting because the failure mode of a bad extraction is not a syntax error.
  • Injecting the regression found a vacuous check, again. The unknown-ripple-action assertion ran before any source was selected, so there was no proc.rippledetector to forward to and no echo could ever appear. It passed for a reason unrelated to the code under test. It now runs after connect and after play, with a positive control that a KNOWN action does echo.

68. WHAT THE CLOCK SAYS, AND WHAT THE HOST BELIEVES (2026-08-29)#

Finishing #61/#62/#63 and running #52 configuration 3. Four defects, three of them found by running the thing rather than reading it — which is the through-line of the day.

68a. The throughput check could not fire on the profiles that record everything#

Measuring #63's time-in-session decay on PXIe + NP1.0 — a profile with no channels: list — the backend reported:

interp.npx.ap throughput 12904.60% of nominal (38706192 records in 10.0 s)

129× too high, and 129 is exactly (6 + 2·384) / 6. An empty filter means "write the whole row" (Npx.cpp: if (filter.empty()) writes sampleDimensions() channels), but the check passed activeRecordIndices.size() — zero — into recordFrameBytes, which computed a frame consisting of nothing but its header.

The silly number is not the harm. Being inflated 129× it sat permanently above the 95 % threshold, so the check took the reassuring branch every single time. A band on one of these profiles could have dropped ninety percent of its samples and still reported 1290 %. The check was structurally incapable of firing on Pxie.yaml and OneBox.yaml — the probe-agnostic profiles, which are the defaults.

The sidecar had it right all along, and recordFrameBytes' own doc comment had predicted the failure:

"If those two ever disagreed, the check would report a shortfall that was really its own arithmetic error … do not let them separate again."

They separated. Telling the next person not to duplicate a rule is weaker than not having two of it, so the rule is now resolvePayloadChannels(), called by both, and unit-tested. After the fix the same recording reads 100.00 %.

68b. #63's continuity check — and why it is a span, not a step#

The remaining half of #63 was to say when samples were lost, where the size ratio says only how many. The design was settled by measurement rather than by preference. Real hardware, 10 s, PXIe + NP1.0:

band tick deltas observed negative steps ticks/frame
ap −8, −7, +4, +5 25 003 of 300 048 3.3333
lfp 39, 40, 41 0 39.9996

The AP stamps advance inside each 12-sample IMEC packet and then step back, netting exactly 40 ticks per packet. A per-frame "did the clock advance" test would fire twenty-five thousand times on a perfectly healthy ten-second recording — and this codebase already says a false alarm is worse than no alarm, because the next real one gets ignored.

The span is exact on both bands, so the check compares frames written against what the batch's own tick span implies. Exact even where the per-frame deltas are not monotonic, localised to a tick position, and two loads plus two stores per batch — nothing per sample. Validated on hardware across four bands and two recordings: zero false alarms, and the arithmetic matches to under one frame. The gap-detection maths is a pure function with five unit cases, four of them built from real measured spans.

68c. A Disconnect left the backend believing a device was still ready#

Running #52 configuration 3 with both basestations attached, selecting the OneBox profile (the probe is on the PXIe, so it must fail) returned:

Still connected to "". Disconnect before selecting another source.

Still connected to nothing. teardownSource() cleared mSourceName and broadcast stateSource:"" but never called setDeviceState(false), so mDeviceReady stayed true. onSourceSelection reads that as hadLiveSource, so the next connect to fail for any reason was reported as a phantom conflict with an empty source name — and then restored that non-existent source with setDeviceState(true).

The real cause was masked completely. After the fix the same connect says what is actually wrong: No Headstage detected on any port!

It reproduces in sim, so it never needed hardware to find — only somebody to disconnect and then fail a connect. Covered in command_surface.py.

68d. #52 configuration 3 — run, 9/9#

The rig turned out to have both basestations attached (1 PXIe module and 1 OneBox, ambiguous: true), which is configuration 3 — the fullest of the five and the one never run. All nine checks pass:

  • enumeration sees both, and reports itself ambiguous;
  • a probe-agnostic profile asks which basestation instead of guessing, tagged kind: "basestation-choice" so the frontend renders a picker rather than an error;
  • selecting PXIe with both attached connects and streams the PXIe;
  • selecting OneBox fails for the right reason — a missing probe — which is what proves the profile reached the box it named rather than the first one found;
  • and the PXIe still connects afterwards, so closing one did not disturb the other.

One check was wrong before the code was: it looked for an event named basestation-choice, when the ask is a guiStatus carrying a __BASESTATIONS__ sentinel. It reported a working mechanism as broken. Read the payload, not the event name.

68e. #61 / #62 — held the audio device fixed, and the ear is the same to 0.04 ms#

Yesterday's NP1.0/PXIe row could only say the ear differed because the default audio device differed. Sweeping every output device on the rig turns that into a controlled comparison:

host API device measured ring raw → ear §2, NP2.0/OneBox
MME Sound Mapper (default) 98.08 ms 16.96 ms ~116.04 ms ~116.0 ms
WDM-KS SPDIF Out 8.71 ms 3.04 ms ~12.7 ms —
DirectSound Primary Sound Driver 13.79 ms 33.33 ms ~48.1 ms ~36.9 ms

On the device both original sessions defaulted to, the ear is 116.04 ms against 116.0 ms — forty microseconds apart, across a different probe generation and a different transport. source_lag is 1.00 ms on every device that opened, on both rigs.

Between the two measured rows the experiment has now varied the probe generation, the transport, the band path and the audio device, and the two terms this system owns — source lag and frame interval — did not move: 1.00 ms and ~19 ms in every combination. The two remaining cells vary nothing that has not already been varied. Run them when a probe is being swapped anyway; a swap of their own buys a result that is already implied.

The machine's device list has also changed since 2026-08-25 (six outputs, not nine; the NVIDIA/monitor entries are gone), so §2's indices do not correspond. Match rows by host API and device name.

68f. #63's decay does not reproduce here#

The second machine saw a band fall from 99.99 % at t+15 s to 65.8 % at t+90 s with the ADC disabled. On this rig, PXIe + NP1.0, 384 channels, same protocol:

t+15 s t+90 s
ap 100.00 % 100.00 %
lfp 100.00 % 100.10 %

Both bands also report continuous against the probe clock. So the decay is not general — it did not reproduce on a different transport on the original machine. It remains unattributed between "that machine" and "the OneBox path"; separating those needs a probe on the OneBox, which is a physical move.

68g. Channels recorded by default — confirmed, and now enforced#

Asked directly, so audited across all 35 configs and verified against a real recording rather than only the YAML.

Every hardware profile the source picker offers records all 384 probe channels — Neuropixels, OneBox, PXIe, PXIe + NI-DAQ, PXIe with NI-DAQ. A recording block with no channels: key records the whole row, and none of them has one. The PXIe + NP1.0 recording above writes 774-byte frames = 6 + 2·384, which is the confirmation that matters.

The adc / nidaq / nidaq_di lists ([0..11], [0..7]) look like restrictions but are complete: those devices have exactly that many channels.

The three-channel profiles are deliberate and must stay. NPX 1.0, NPX 2.0 Multishank, the OneBox and ASAP variants and the two latency benches record [1, 2, 3] or [1, 192, 196]. They are gui_hidden and exist for latency benches and #63's ADC-throughput control, whose published 78.1 % / 99.99 % pair is only reproducible against the three-channel pair. They remain selectable by exact name, which is how the OneBox latency matrix drove them — so a script that names one gets three channels by design.

Now enforced by python/tests/test_config_channels.py, which fails if an operator-facing profile ever acquires a channels: list, and separately if a NEW profile records a subset without being declared exempt. The failure it guards is the worst kind available: a narrowed recording looks entirely normal afterwards — the sidecar honestly describes three columns, every converter honestly exports three — and the channels that were never written cannot be recovered.

68h. The both-attached check is now symmetric#

The first version hard-coded "PXIe has the probe, OneBox must fail", which is only today's wiring. ripple_tools/verify_basestation_pair.py now discovers which basestation holds the probe and asserts the pair: exactly one connects and streams, and the empty one fails for the honest reason. Move the probe to the other box and the same script verifies the mirror with no edit — 10/10 as run.

The mirror is structural rather than coincidental: "No Headstage detected on any port!" is raised in the shared NpInterface base (NpInterface.cpp:1907). Only detectProbePort() is platform-specific — PXIe gates on np_openPort then np_detectHeadStage, the OneBox overrides it to call np_detectHeadStage directly over its two ports — and both failures converge on the same reporting path.

69. A FILTER IS NOT A BRICK WALL (2026-08-31)#

Started as a complaint about a figure and ended in the signal model. The operator looked at the heatmap page in the user docs and asked why the report showed the AP view when the heatmap presumably showed LFP. Both halves of the answer were defects.

69a. The figure was comparing two unrelated things#

user_docs/images/heatmap_column.png was shot by hand, and tests/gui/heatmap_view.py only ever sets column 0. So the left column was an LFP heatmap of 384 channels and the right column was whatever the source defaults to — the AP band, 20 channels, e20–e39. The caption said "Right: the same source as traces", which is literally true and reads as the same data drawn two ways — contradicting the feature's actual claim, that a heatmap shows the same channels in the same order.

Both figures are now a product of the test (python tests/gui/heatmap_view.py --figures), which sets both columns explicitly. A hand-shot figure has no way to be wrong-and-noticed.

The claim itself was also asserted in prose and nowhere checked. It is now an assertion: at a given density with shanks unmerged, heatmapChannels() returns visibleChannels.slice(), so the test compares the two lists elementwise.

69b. "Attenuates" had been implemented as "eliminates"#

The operator's real point: a 300 Hz high-pass does not remove a 150–250 Hz oscillation, so ripples must appear in both bands. The simulator injected the burst into the LFP band only, justified by a comment in generateApSample that got the premise right and the conclusion wrong.

A first-order 300 Hz corner passes 0.5547 of a 200 Hz signal — −5.1 dB. Relative to the LFP band (itself down 0.9285 from its own 500 Hz low-pass) the AP band carries 0.597.

The corner is not a guess: the vendor API exposes exactly one control for it, setAPCornerFrequency(..., bool disable_high_pass), documented as "true for disabling the 300Hz high-pass cut-off filter". The filter ORDER is an assumption — imec publishes corners, not orders — and first order is both the conservative choice (a steeper filter attenuates more) and the physically likely one for a per-channel stage repeated 384 times.

69c. And the two simulators disagreed by 15×#

Chasing 69b turned up the other half. scripts/gen_synthetic_ripples.py did filter the AP band — with sosfiltfilt(butter(4, 300, 'high')). Zero-phase filtering applies the response twice, so a 4th-order design rolled off like an 8th-order one. Measured: 0.0375.

path AP gain at 200 Hz
live simulator (before) 0 — no ripple injected at all
play-file generator (before) 0.0375
both (now) 0.5547

Both files said "as on real hardware". Neither number was reachable from the other file, and each path was internally consistent, so nothing could go red.

filtfilt is an analysis tool, not a hardware model. It doubles the effective order and is acausal; a probe can do neither.

69d. What was actually built#

  • src/modules/neuropixels/SimBandResponse.hpp — the model, free of Qt and the vendor API so it is cheap to test. tests/test_simbandresponse.cpp, 7 cases.
  • scripts/np1_band_model.py — the Python half, deliberately separate from the generator, which imports h5py and therefore cannot be imported by the gate's Python. That is not hypothetical: it is precisely why the drift survived.
  • python/tests/test_band_response.py — pins the same numbers, asserts the filter is causal (an impulse test sosfiltfilt fails), and parses the constants out of the C++ header so changing one side alone goes red.
  • rippleAmplitude is now defined as the burst peak in the LFP band, so the LFP band — and every validated detector threshold — is bit-for-bit unchanged. The AP band gains signal; nothing loses it.

Verified end to end on a recording, not just in unit tests:

measured expected
AP burst amplitude 59.5 counts 59.7 (0.597 × 100)
AP / LFP ratio 0.582 0.597
burst interval 2.00 s 2.0
AP burst over its own in-band noise floor 43× —

The ratio scatters while the amplitude does not, because the LFP band's in-band noise floor is 3.5× the AP band's (4.99 vs 1.41 counts): same per-sample noise, a twelfth of the bandwidth.

69e. Two traps that cost real time, now written down#

Both are in developer_docs/AgentTraps.md:

  • Recording .bin frames carry a 6-byte header. np.fromfile(...).reshape(-1, nch) produces garbage that looks like data — full-scale rails, plausible "bursts" at almost-right spacing. The tell is amplitude: simulated counts should be in the hundreds, not railing at ±32768.
  • Revert the regression, then REBUILD. Proving a test can fail leaves the regression in bin/SpikesPeak.exe after the source is clean. A record-and-measure cycle was spent measuring a brick-wall binary; the analysis was right and the binary was stale.

69f. Time bases, and a dock that leaves#

Three operator requests came out of the same session, all about ambiguity the figure exposed.

The heatmap's span was a hardcoded 8 s (HEATMAP_WINDOW_SEC; heatmapWindowSec was never assigned) with no control and no label, while trace columns follow the toolbar's control, default 1 s. Two columns of the same probe, x axes differing by 8×, nothing on screen saying so. Now: the span is selectable per column, and every column names its own time base in the readout — in both view modes, because a label that appears in only one mode is a label nobody learns to look for.

The readout is one element with a re-applied suffix rather than a sibling span: .page-info is grid-area: depth, so a second element with that class would have rendered on top of the depth readout.

Toolbar "Window" → "Time". Since detached windows landed, "Window" read as "which window".

The ripple dock detaches. One ⧉ button in the dock header, which becomes ⧉ Dock in the detached window — one control, two directions, in the place the operator already found it. The detached window is ?view=ripple and builds zero ChartColumns: Chrome allows ~16 WebGL contexts per renderer process and force-loses the oldest silently, so contexts allocated in a window that never shows them would blank a live view the operator is watching. It registers as its own ripple role so it does not take a number in the operator's "View 2 of 3". Closing it by any route — redock, the X, a crashed tab — restores the in-page dock, because the main window keys on the peer set rather than on the redock message.

70. THE FRONT DOOR, AND THE RULES THAT KEEP IT FROM ROTTING AGAIN (2026-08-31)#

A documentation pass across the whole book, run as four workstreams in parallel over disjoint file sets so no two writers touched the same page. This section records the entry documents set -- README.md, CLAUDE.md, developer_docs/README.md, the new developer_docs/Contributing.md, scripts/build_docs.py, and the two finished OneBox briefs -- plus the rules the whole pass adopts. The other three workstreams are recorded by their own edits.

70a. The public front page was a note addressed to an agent#

README.md was 616 bytes. Line 5 was a bracketed TODO -- "re-capture this screenshot..." -- with the image markdown nested inside the parenthesis, so the picture never rendered and the note did. That was what https://github.com/NeuralAscension/SpikesPeak showed a visitor: a tagline, an instruction to an AI, and an architecture diagram.

It is now a front door written for a human: what the system is, the hardware it drives, a quick start (build.bat --no-pause then run_spikespeak.bat --no-browser then http://localhost:8234), where the four doc entry points are, and the issue-tracker URL. No page in this repo said where issues live, while the docs cite #11/#16/#18/#34/#49/#51/#59/#65/#69/#70 throughout.

The screenshot problem itself is real and was not deleted, only moved: images/maindisplay.PNG is the July build with a SciChart watermark burned into all four live-view columns, and SciChart was removed on 2026-08-27. It is now an item in developer_docs/NextTask_DocsRefresh.md with the recapture recipe attached, rather than a note on the front page.

There was also no CONTRIBUTING-style page anywhere -- find . -iname "CONTRIBUTING*" hit only vendored rapidyaml. developer_docs/Contributing.md is new: branch and commit style as practiced, the agent rule (do not git add/commit/push unless the operator asks) beside the human flow, the gate as a pointer rather than a number, and the doc conventions.

70b. Three documents, three case counts, all three wrong#

CLAUDE.md said the C++ suite was "~1.6 s, 316 cases"; Testing.md said 414. Both are documents a newcomer is told to read first, so the two mandatory entry points disagreed with each other before the tree was touched. CLAUDE.md also said "13 tests" for the GUI suite and "121 tests" for the Python suite. Ground truth on 2026-08-31: 26 registered C++ suites, 22 GUI tests, 126 Python tests.

Updating three numbers would have bought a few weeks. The fix is structural: counts live in Testing.md and nowhere else, and every other page quotes the file. CLAUDE.md and developer_docs/README.md now say so explicitly, and the hand-back checklist carries it as a rule.

Two related corrections in the same file: CLAUDE.md said "until BOTH suites pass" while the checklist three lines below listed three and the next heading read "One command for all three"; and every build.bat invocation now carries --no-pause. That trap was recorded in 64c and 65a -- it hangs a scripted chain at "Press any key to continue" -- and had never been promoted into the file agents actually read.

70c. The map pointed a newcomer at a finished experiment#

developer_docs/README.md listed NextTask_OneBoxClosedLoopLatency.md as "NEXT UP" and NextTask_OneBoxLatencyMatrix.md as "Ready to run". Both completed on 2026-08-20 -- section 48, the full 3x2 matrix on both probes, 11,000 detections / 11,000 confirmed fires / 0 unmatched. Anyone following the map would have spent a session of rig time, in saline with a live stimulator, re-running a matrix that was already in the log.

Both are now in developer_docs/archive/, each with a dated DONE banner and its whole body kept: the positional-recdir trap, inner-vs-outer directory, "do not pipe the runner into tail" (a shell pipeline exits with the status of its last member, which is how five cells were "analyzed" without producing a number), gate-vs-arm, and --stim-band adc --stim-channel N. None of that is specific to the matrix, which is why the files stay. Two corrections went in with the move: the wiring snapshot is marked as the 2026-08-24 bench with OneBoxWiring.md section 4 named as the current authority (it shows the SMA on IO9, two rewires ago), and a dead cross-reference to "Recording.md section 5" for the pulse-width note now points at LatencyProfiling.md, where it actually is.

Other reference-layer fixes on the same page: the banner was dated 2026-07-21 and named in-vivo as the next milestone, which passed on 2026-08-28 (section 65) -- and it predated the entire OneBox transport (sections 48, 51, 55) although the working branch is called feature/OneBoxSupport. The green box said "Both suites" over a two-command block and then claimed CLAUDE.md "carries the same checklist", which gave a newcomer an explicit reason not to cross-check and to skip the Python suite that covers the converters and the sync pairing every published latency passes through. The Windows quick start was textually corrupted -- exe -> binrun_spikespeak.bat, two commands fused by a missing newline. "A fresh clone needs no setup" (said twice) is now qualified with the two things the same page's own dependency table says are needed: HDF5 on CMAKE_PREFIX_PATH, and internet at first configure for PortAudio and rapidyaml. And the hardware table said slot/port/dock were hard-coded when they are detected at runtime by BasestationScan + NeuropixelsSource::discoverProbeHardware(); it also had no OneBox row at all.

One entry was actively harmful. "Working in parallel" listed webinterface/index.html #selectSource as a shared hotspot needing "one option per source" -- following it writes dead markup into the very file that warns against it, whose own comment reads "Do not add profiles here". The <select> is a hidden two-entry pre-connect fallback; the real list is rebuilt by main.js rebuildSourceOptions() from the backend's configList.

70d. Eight pages were rendering under "Unsorted"#

DetachedViews.md, HeatmapView.md, IntanOpenQuestions.md, OneBoxWiring.md, RendererContract.md, SecondMachineRunbooks.md, SimdDispatch.md and SimulatedSignals.md were in no GROUPS entry in scripts/build_docs.py, so the book filed them at the bottom of the nav -- which the README itself calls "where docs go to be missed". Three GROUPS entries pointed at files that had already moved to archive/, and two pages (AudioVideoLatency.md, CodeStyle.md) were in one group in the README and a different one in GROUPS. Building.md was in GROUPS with no README row at all.

All nine pages (the eight plus the new Contributing.md) are now grouped in both places, the stale entries are gone, and the two disagreements are resolved. The book builds with no not-in-GROUPS warning and no missing-file warning. One row was also factually wrong and is corrected: RippleDetectorThreading.md does not cover "why the stim socket/ACK path is thread-safe" -- it is a measured per-electrode threading trade-off report (~28 ns per channel-sample; one thread covers ~14,000 channels; the whole probe costs 2.7 % of a core).

70e. The rules, which are the point#

The individual corrections above will be re-earned by the next six months of work. These four are what stops that:

  1. Suite and case counts live in developer_docs/Testing.md and nowhere else. Quote the file, never the number. Three files each carried their own on 2026-08-31 and all three were wrong.
  2. A finished NextTask_* brief is archived in the commit that finishes it -- not in a later cleanup pass. A completed brief left under "Plans and open briefs" reads as work waiting to be done, and on this repo that costs rig time.
  3. A page making a "current behavior" claim carries the date it was checked. An undated claim outlives the behavior it describes and there is then no way to tell which.
  4. A page's README table row and its scripts/build_docs.py GROUPS entry move in the same commit. They are the same list twice -- the map a human reads and the map the book renders -- and two maps that disagree are worse than one.

CLAUDE.md gained one more repo convention on the same principle: user_docs/ goes stale with the toolbar, so a change to anything an operator clicks checks user_docs/README.md's toolbar table and user_docs/GUI-Guide.md's screenshots in the same change. CLAUDE.md had never mentioned user_docs/ at all, which is why those pages have been repeatedly refreshed after the fact rather than with the change that broke them.

70. TWO HARDWARE QUESTIONS, ANSWERED AND CLOSED (2026-08-31, night)#

NP2.0 multishank moved onto the OneBox by the operator, with SMA 1 patched to IO11 and IO11 shorted to IO0, the Pi's stim trigger on IO2, and a 1 Hz / 50 % duty / 5 V square on the SMA. That wiring answered both open OneBox questions in one session.

70a. #68 — the ADC stream costs nothing, and probably never did#

The four-cell matrix ({with ADC, no ADC} × {t+15 s, t+90 s}, 10 s recordings, both configs selected by exact name within a single acquisition):

cell band records in 10 s throughput continuity
with ADC, t+15 raw 300 045 100.0 % continuous
with ADC, t+15 adc 303 066 100.0 % continuous
with ADC, t+90 raw 300 000 100.0 % continuous
no ADC, t+15 raw 299 956 100.0 % continuous
no ADC, t+90 raw 299 996 100.0 % continuous

Against the claim of 78.1 % with ADC / 99.99 % without.

Neither the cost nor the time-in-session decay exists here, and the ADC band is itself at 100 % — so it is not the ADC under-delivering to protect the probe.

The issue's question was whether the effect belonged to the OneBox/FT601 path or to that machine. The answer turned out to be neither: the second machine had already failed to reproduce it on 2026-08-26 (4/4 at 100 %), so it does not reproduce on the machine that produced it. Closed on the operator's call, with the likeliest explanation being a measurement artifact — the original numbers predate both the sidecar-frame-width counting method and the continuity check, and #63's own follow-up runbook had to name "the trap that ruined the first attempt".

What makes closing safe rather than optimistic: every record stop now prints throughput AND continuity, the latter checked against the probe's own clock over an explicit tick span. If the effect returns it announces itself on any machine, with nobody needing to remember the issue.

70b. #67 — the comparator band is correct, and the short is what proved it#

12.5 s recording of raw + adc + adc.di.

  • The comparator samples are exactly {0, 1}, across 377 279 samples × 12 channels. This was the unknown that gated the design: Open Ephys shifts them into an event word without normalising, which is only correct if they are 0/1, and the vendor documentation never says.
  • IO11 vs IO0 — one electrical node, two channels at opposite ends of the array — agreed on 377 276 / 377 279 samples (99.9992 %). All three disagreements are within two samples of an edge (metastability on a shared node); zero away from an edge. The characteristic failure of unpacking the last 12 of 24 samples is a stride error putting the signal on channel N±1, and it is invisible with one signal on one channel. The short makes it decidable.
  • No other channel moves at all — 0 transitions on the other ten, and nothing on IO1 or IO10.
  • Half-period 500.07 ms against 500.0 nominal.
  • The analog twin is not railed: 4.960 V peak, 0 samples at ≥98 % of full scale.

That last one needed adc_voltage_range: 10, added to the profile this session. At the ±5 V default a 5 V input sits at exactly full scale (5.0 × 6553.6 = 32768, one past int16 max), so the analog would have clipped flat while the comparator bit stayed perfectly correct — a failure invisible in precisely the band under test.

70c. Method notes worth keeping#

  • Numbers come from the backend's own verdict lines, never from the driving script's clock.
  • The control profile was not regenerated from OneBox.yaml: that records 384 channels where the Npx2* pair records three, a difference large enough to swamp the effect being measured.
  • Both ages were taken inside one acquisition, so the time-in-session comparison has no reconnect in it.
  • One bug caught mid-analysis: spikespeak_io.read_band's glob *_adc_* also matches *_adc_di_*, so asking for the analog band could silently return the digital one. It now confirms the band from the sidecar — the same failure mode as the frame-header trap, closed the same way.
  • Every shutdown was a graceful quit. A force-kill with a real OneBox attached leaves the FT601 handle claimed and costs a physical power-cycle.

Runners: ripple_tools/adc_throughput_matrix.py, ripple_tools/check_digital_in.py. Artifacts under RecordingTests/2026-08-31_{adc_matrix,digital_in}/ — the .bin files are not committed.

71. THE SWR DOCK LOOKED LIKE A DIFFERENT APPLICATION (2026-09-01)#

The operator, having just seen the new Convert dock: "I do like the aesthetic of this converter dock with regards to the background better than the aesthetic of the ripple detector dock ... change the background aesthetic of the SWR detector dock and the color scheme of it to sort of match the converter dock such that the SWR detector dock is more similar aesthetically to the GUI itself, which right now I just don't think it is."

71a. What was actually wrong#

Not "the colors are ugly" — the dock was using green as its general voice. Headings, the title, the column labels and the drop target were all green, with amber #FFD54F parentheticals on top for unit hints. Amber is the GUI's warning color, so the one thing it was applied to — text that merely says "all values in seconds" — read as a caution. Beside the rest of the GUI, whose chrome is neutral grey and which spends color only on state, the dock read as a bolted-on tool.

The rule adopted, and written into RippleModule.js as a PALETTE NOTE so it survives the next edit:

Green for function only. The Start button, a selected-channel chip, the drag target, the armed tab, and the detecting/STIM state. An accent that marks everything marks nothing.

Header #222→#2c2c2c with a near-white title, section labels #cdeccd→#8d979e, unit hints #FFD54F→#7f8a86, cards #2f2f2f→#2a2a2a, inputs and buttons onto the Convert dock's #33393b/#4a5254. The idle drop target lost its green tint and keeps it only on dragover.

The last one was the least obvious and the most clearly right: .rd-thr, the threshold readout, was a green box that never changes on detection. It encodes nothing, so under the rule above it has no claim on the accent — and inside a red .rd-col.detect column a green box actively misreads.

71b. The screenshots were already two generations stale#

Restyling the dock meant checking user_docs/, and every ripple screenshot there was already wrong before this change: they still showed "◉ Ripple Detection", from before the rename to SWR Detector. They were hand-shot, so nothing had ever told anyone they had drifted.

All eight in-use frames were regenerated from live sim sessions by a scratchpad driver (not committed — it is a docs asset generator, not a test). Two further images, np2_livez.png and np2_training.png, were referenced from nothing and were deleted: an unreferenced screenshot of an old UI is pure liability, because the next person to need one will reach for it.

71c. Regenerating them found three factual errors in the prose#

This is the part worth keeping. Re-shooting a screenshot forces you to reproduce the state the caption claims, and the caption is where the rot was:

  1. The live-z figures were invented. The text said e0's threshold "jumped from ~45 to ~69". The real numbers are 34.3 → 81.9. Neither endpoint was close.
  2. The at-a-glance frame was captioned with a state its own settings forbade. It claimed three channels in ● RIPPLE — but was shot at quorum 1, and at quorum 1 the detector reports only the first channel to cross; the rest are suppressed by the lockout. Eleven minutes were spent retrying for a frame the parameters made unreachable before that was understood. Reshot at quorum 3 it genuinely reads 3/3 ch 0,10,20. That behaviour is now in the parameter table in user_docs/RippleDetection.md, because it is exactly the thing an operator would otherwise chase.
  3. The NP2 caption named the wrong channels (2/3 ch 50,100; the run gives 2/3 ch 0,50), and SimulatedData.md described "RAW band, 2 s window, 600 µV" for a frame that is a dock-only crop and has never contained the main view at all.

Also corrected while there: the NP1 at-a-glance frame had been shot on the AP band under a caption that says the main view is on the LFP band "where NP1 ripples live".

71d. The method note#

A caption is an assertion, and re-shooting is how you test it. Every one of the errors above survived because the image and the sentence were maintained separately, by hand, years apart. The driver reaches each state the way an operator does — add channels, set z, press Start, wait for training to actually report complete — and prints the values it read, so the captions are written from the run rather than from memory.

One trick worth reusing: the ● RIPPLE state is held for lockout (0.200 s) and the burst is ~100 ms of every 2 s, so polling-then-shooting loses the race nearly every time. The driver pushes detectUntil far into the future at the instant it observes the state, which extends the display hold only — the lockout parameter visible in the frame still reads 0.200. Freezing on every poll instead was wrong and produced a frame that could not occur: two red columns beside a cowsay reading 1/3, because detections seconds apart had been accumulated into one picture.

71e. Then every frame was checked against its caption, and found five more#

Regenerating the images fixed the images. It did not prove the prose was right, so each of the eight frames was re-read against every sentence that captions it, and each claimed mismatch was handed to a second reader told to refute it. Eight shots, five confirmed, three refuted.

The three refusals matter as much as the confirmations — one "mismatch" was a caption belonging to a neighbouring image, one was a default column in a parameter table rather than a description of the picture, and one was a quick-start instruction the frame deliberately precedes. A single-pass check would have "fixed" all three into being wrong.

What survived:

  1. A caption written in this very session was wrong. Describing NP2's e100 as "its envelope is climbing but has not crossed" — measured on the pixels, e100's envelope crosses the dashed threshold at ~87% across its plot and ends ~32 px above it in a 70 px box, the same excursion as the two columns the caption calls detections. Written from the on-screen label, not from the picture. See below for why the label says what it says.
  2. Two captions promised a stimulation that provably did not happen. "...and the stim fires" / "the stimulation trigger fires" — while in both frames enable stimulation is unchecked, the on-screen hint beside it reads "detection only, nothing is transmitted", the server pill reads disabled, and the STIM LED is unlit. The docs were telling an operator that a rig configured to transmit nothing had transmitted.
  3. A tip bound itself to the screenshots and then disagreed with them. "a ~2 s Window and a ~600 µV Scale ... that's what the screenshots below use" — the frames were shot at 1 s and 300 µV, exactly half on both axes. Fixed by re-shooting at the advised values rather than by softening the tip, so the advice is now demonstrated by the picture beneath it.

71f. The label that asserts what the graph disproves#

Finding 1 is not a documentation bug underneath. _colStatus() returns the literal string "below threshold" whenever a channel is trained and c.detecting is false — and c.detecting is set only from the backend's onDetected, which names the electrodes in the reported detection. A channel that crosses just outside the coincidence window is therefore labelled below threshold while the sparkline directly above it, drawn from the live envelope against that same threshold, shows it plainly above.

It is this repo's recurring defect class inverted: not announcing requested state instead of confirmed state, but announcing a claim about the signal that the panel's own graph refutes. The label should say what is actually true — "not part of a detection" — or be derived from the envelope so the two cannot disagree. Flagged as a follow-up rather than changed here: it is operator-facing text quoted in two user docs and pictured in a committed screenshot, so the wording is the operator's call. user_docs/RippleDetection.md §5 now explains the apparent contradiction in the meantime.

71g. And the dock could not count#

Checking the frames also turned up that the dock's own section headings read 1 · Channels, 2 · Parameters, Per channel — … and 4 · Detection & stimulation. It numbered 1, 2, _, 4. Both user docs refer to that third block as section ② — so the docs were right and the UI had simply lost the number. Now 3 · Per channel — z-score threshold & live envelope, with the reason in a comment beside it so it is not "tidied" away again.

Nothing in the test suites asserted on that string, which is why it survived; it is the kind of thing only a person or a caption-checker looking at the rendered panel would ever notice.

71h. The re-check came back clean#

After the fixes and a final re-shoot, the same eight-frames-against-their-captions pass was run again: 0 confirmed mismatches, with three fresh claims raised and all three refuted on review — each was a quick-start suggesting a 3 s training time being read as a description of a frame that shows the 10 s default. Advice is not a caption.

Two things this cost, worth knowing before repeating it: the frames had to be re-shot four times, because each fix found in the pictures (the band, the quorum, the window/scale, the section number) invalidated the pictures. Do the reading first. And the play-file run died once on a transient the browser closed the CDP connection — re-running it was enough, but the two play-file frames are the ones to check first if a future run looks stale.

The generator and its logs stayed in the session scratchpad. Nothing about this needed a scratch file in the repo.

72. SCREENSHOTS NOW CARRY THEIR DATE (2026-09-01)#

Operator, after §71: "Could you actually go through all the screenshots and rename them to include the date? This way we'll know if/when we need to update a screenshot."

The right instinct, and §71 is the evidence: a hand-shot screenshot is the only artifact here with no way to say how old it is. Prose can be read against the code, a test goes red, a derived fact recomputes — a picture just sits there looking plausible. Ten of them had been showing a dock renamed days earlier and nobody could tell.

49 screenshots renamed to <name>_YYYY-MM-DD.<ext>, and 97 references across 19 documents rewritten. Docs book rebuilds at 0 broken links.

72a. The date is not today#

Each file took the date it last actually changed, from git log -1 --date=short; only files with uncommitted changes took today's. Stamping the whole set with today would have destroyed exactly the signal being added. The spread is the payoff — facts.py now reports it directly:

oldest 2026-07-07   2026-07 x7   2026-08 x28   2026-09 x14

Seven images are two months old. That was previously unknowable without opening each one.

72b. Three things the tooling caught that a careful person would not have#

  1. Two basenames were not unique. frames.png and load_cpu_gpu.png live in both benchmarks/plots/ and developer_docs/images/renderer/. A basename-level rewrite — the obvious implementation — would have silently repointed the benchmark pages at the renderer's figures.
