Those two are matched parent-qualified; the tool refuses outright if that is still ambiguous.
  2. The reference regex matched 16 of 97. Its left boundary forbade a preceding /, and every markdown reference is a path. Left alone it would have renamed 49 files and left the docs pointing at nothing — and the rename itself would have looked like it worked.
  3. Runner-written images must NOT be dated, and they are not where you would guess. A date baked into a name a runner writes is undone on the next run, which recreates the undated twin and leaves the docs pointing at whichever nobody refreshes. The first exemption was the directory developer_docs/images/gui/ — wrong the day it was written: a GUI test writes user_docs/images/heatmap_column.png and a benchmark script writes developer_docs/images/threading_sweep_time.png. Four images, in two directories the rule did not cover.

The rule that is actually right: if code names the file, code owns its freshness. The exemption is now derived by scanning the tree's own source for the basename as a string literal.

72c. A date in a filename is a written fact, so it is checked#

Per §1 of KnowledgeOps.md — written facts rot. The very next person to regenerate an image will overwrite the pixels and leave the old date sitting there, which is worse than no date, because it reads as a guarantee. python/tests/test_screenshot_dates.py holds three checks:
R1 every hand-maintained screenshot is dated
R2 a screenshot edited in the working tree carries today's date
R3 the runner-directory exemption still has a runner writing to it

All three were proved able to fail, and R1 initially could not. It read git ls-files, so an undated image dropped into user_docs/images/ was invisible until after it was committed — a check that waits for the commit is a code review, not a guard. It now reads --cached --others --exclude-standard and fires the moment the file exists.

R2 also had to learn that a pure rename is not an edit: git status reports R, the pixels are byte-identical, and the whole 49-file backfill would otherwise have failed its own new rule.

Ages are queryable with python scripts/facts.py --only screenshots. The convention is written up in developer_docs/KnowledgeOps.md §8, next to the decision table that sends people there.

73. AUDITING THREE DOCS AGAINST THE CODE FOUND TWO LIVE BUGS (2026-09-01)#

Operator: "Ensure the probe heat map and detached view things are also thoroughly documented. As well as the most recent profiling of the code — the profiling should be overwritten every time as newer versions are being made."

Each of HeatmapView.md, DetachedViews.md, ProbeActivityHeatmap.md and Profiling.md was read against the code it describes, and every claimed gap handed to a second reader told to refute it. 19 confirmed, 11 refuted. The refutations mattered as much as the findings — see §73d.

73a. Two of the "documentation gaps" were shipped defects#

The heatmap span selector was never placed in the grid. .cc-hmwin had a class in ChartColumn.js and no grid-area anywhere, so since 2026-08-31 it had been the one control in the band being auto-placed — which in a named-area grid means an implicit row. That is exactly the failure HeatmapView.md §10 exists to warn about, in the control added five days after the warning was written.

And the first fix was itself incomplete. Two of the eight heatmap area maps were updated and six were missed — the same trap, inside the same hour, while writing the section about it.

Detached windows have been showing the full module strip. The rule meant to hide them read .detached-view .tb-modules, #ripple-toggle, #audio-toggle — none of those three selectors exists in the tree; the container is #module-tabs. So a detached view has been offering a second SWR Detector — the dock that arms a stimulator — while DetachedViews.md §3 said it "loses the module docks". Now hides the container (any list of tab ids is a list the next module falsifies), and tests/gui/detached_view.py asserts it. Proved by restoring the old selector and watching the new assertion report modules: 'visible'.

A hiding rule with no assertion is a comment. Both defects were invisible to the entire gate because nothing tested the absence of a thing.

73b. Third occurrence, so the rule got mechanized#

§10 said the unplaced-control trap "bit twice". .cc-hmwin made three, and CLAUDE.md's rule is that the second occurrence means writing the rule rather than fixing the bug. python/tests/test_column_grid.py now asserts the rule, not the instance:
G1 every cc-* class the frontend sets has a grid-area
G2 every area named in a --cc-areas map is defined by some rule
G3 every template declares as many tracks as its widest area row uses

All three proved able to fail; G1 reproduces the real bug exactly. G3 is what caught the incomplete fix — which is the argument for it existing, made by the thing itself on its first day.

73c. Profiling: overwritten in place, and one conclusion reversed#

Profiling.md now opens by saying it is overwritten, never appended to — the opposite of this file, deliberately: Status answers what happened; Profiling answers what is true of the build that ships, and a superseded performance number is not history, it is a wrong answer that still reads like a right one. History is git log -p.

All four measurements were re-run. Three were stable. One reversed:

was (n=2) now (n=6)
SPIKESPEAK_SIMD=avx2 1.022 cores 1.020 (1.011–1.029)
SPIKESPEAK_SIMD=scalar 1.019 cores 1.065 (1.047–1.083)
conclusion "No difference." 4.5 pp, ranges do not overlap

The old number came with an explanation — NP1.0 takes its LFP from the probe, so proc.lfp never runs and the filter barely executes — and the explanation was incomplete, which is worse than the number being wrong. DisplayProcessor.cpp:561 dispatches calcMinMaxAvx2 for the display min/max, which runs on every configuration that draws. The filter was never the only consumer, so there was never a reason to expect zero.

"No difference, and here is the mechanism" is a dangerous shape. The mechanism made a wrong number feel explained, and an explained number is one nobody re-measures. --simd-reps exists now so a claim about this can be made at a rep count that supports it.

Also fixed there: the documented reproduce commands did not run. bench_filter links Qt6::Core and only ctest-registered suites get the Qt PATH injected, so every shell variant in §5 died on Qt6Core.dll. And §1's "real work" column was a hand-derivation from a definition nobody had written down — profile_frontend.py now emits real_work_ms, so the table regenerates instead of being recomputed by whoever edits the page.

73d. What the refutations were worth#

Three examples of the second reader earning its place:

  • I asserted ProbeActivityHeatmap.md was a proposal for a shipped feature and should be archived. Wrong — it is the proposal for the spatial probe-map heatmap, which has not shipped, and the page says so in its first sentence. It does not belong in archive/.
  • The audit claimed the page's "Inferno is the OpenEphys default" premise was wrong. It is not: UI/ColourScheme.cpp:14 is ColourSchemeId::INFERNO. Only our default changed (viridis). Just the true half was corrected.
  • Three "missing" screenshot rules were the parameter table's Default column, which the page already explains is the pre-backend placeholder — reading that footnote is what stopped a correct page being "fixed" into a wrong one.

One refutation was overruled: the Qt-PATH gap was refuted on the grounds that a linked page states the requirement. True, but §5 is a copy-paste reproduce block and the cross-reference sits in a sentence about a different topic. A command block whose commands do not run is broken regardless of what another page says.

74. CONFIGURATION 4 ON THE RIG — PXIe, NO OneBox (2026-09-01)#

Operator switched the NP2.0 multishank probe to the PXIe basestation and powered the OneBox off. Confirmed independently before starting: no FTDI device present on USB, PXI-6133 OK. That is what

52's Configuration 4 asks for — off, not merely unplugged from the probe.#

74a. The four checks#

# check result
1 the basestation scan resolves without ambiguity PASS — {"ambiguous": false, "oneBox": 0, "pxie": 1, "summary": "1 PXIe module"}
2 the dropdown offers no OneBox profile that cannot work PASS — it offers none at all
3 connect / stream / record / disconnect / reconnect PASS
4 all three suites PASS — C++ 26/26, Python 133, GUI 23/23

On check 2. The three refactored profiles — OneBox, PXIe, PXIe + NI-DAQ — are all gui_hidden: true, which looked wrong until the files explained themselves: they are superseded for everyday use by Neuropixels, which resolves the basestation from the scan instead of being told, and are kept as the EXPLICIT OVERRIDE for when a OneBox and a PXIe module are attached at once (auto refuses to guess between them). So the dropdown offering Neuropixels and no OneBox entry is the design working, not a gap.

On check 3, the state machine, which is worth having written down:

at rest      acquisition=no_source
selectSource -> connected  (~8 s)   source.npx.pxi, state=idle, cyclesTotal=0
setPlaying   -> acquiring           state=producing, cyclesTotal 4936 -> 13130
Disconnect   -> no_source, sources=0            (released, and stayed released)
RECONNECT    -> connected  (~8 s)   fresh instance, cyclesTotal back to 0
setPlaying   -> acquiring           4934 -> 13129   <- the same rate as the first connect

The reconnect is the point (#54 is about the second connect). It re-opens and streams at a rate indistinguishable from the first. Recording wrote 232,203,096 B — 300004 frames x 774 B, which is 384 channels of int16 plus the 6-byte header, exactly right for 10 s of NP2.0 at 30 kHz.

74b. Two rounds of the failure being MINE, not the rig's#

The first run reported "the source never opened" and "not committing data" — in the same run that recorded 232 MB of probe data. The contradiction was the tell. wait_for asserted against a state vocabulary I had guessed, before any systemState had been pushed.

The second run reported cyclesTotal 937 -> 937, frozen, while a diagnostic had just watched it climb to 20430. Reading state["latest"] immediately after sending getSystemState returns the PREVIOUS reply; the counter has to be sampled from two states that are genuinely different messages.

Instrument first, assert second. Both rounds were fixed by printing the transitions instead of predicting them. On a rig, a failing check is at least as likely to be a wrong probe as a wrong system — and the cost of believing it is a hardware hunt that finds nothing.

74c. What Config 4 actually found: the single reader refused the recording#

The recording is good. python/spikespeak_io.py — introduced as the single .bin reader, so that two readers could not disagree about a recording — would not open it:

frame is 774 B but 6 + 2*0 = 6 B. Refusing to guess.

The sidecar says num_channels: 0, channels: []. That is not corruption; it is a sentinel the writer documents where it writes it (src/core/RecordingMetadata.cpp): "Empty => the whole block was written (no filter) and the reader infers N from the file size." It is also the common case, because omitting channels: from a profile means record-everything, the safe default every general profile uses — config/Neuropixels.yaml says so in as many words.

So the single reader rejected exactly the recordings an operator is most likely to make, while convert_to_spikeglx.py and convert_to_openephys.py both read the same file without complaint (300004 x 385 int16, and 384 ch / 0 dropped, respectively). Two readers in one repo disagreeing about a documented sentinel is the precise thing that module was created to prevent.

Fixed by honouring the sentinel — there was never anything to guess, frame.bytes gives N directly — while keeping the hard refusal for a NON-empty channel list that contradicts the frame. python/tests/test_reader_all_channels.py pins both directions, because a fix that merely deleted the guard would pass the first and reintroduce the plausible-garbage the guard exists for. Both proved able to fail.

75. THE ARCHITECTURE ARTIFACTS: ONE GENERATOR, NO HAND-DRAWN RIVALS (2026-09-01)#

Operator: "confirm that the architecture files are all fully up-to-date", then "plantuml things can be removed as that's no longer the technique … remove duplicate/older ones."

75a. What was there#

artifact kind state
developer_docs/images/architecture*.svg (37) generated from src/core/Utils.cpp + config/*.yaml, git-ignored current; --check clean
images/architecture.svg + .txt hand-patched PlantUML, embedded by README.md stale
images/plantuml_export.svg + .txt PlantUML class diagram, embedded by the overview stale (last rendered 2026-07-22)
images/Architecture.pdf older export referenced by nothing

The stale one was the front page. images/architecture.svg contained zero occurrences of OneBox, PxieNiDaq, Npx2OneBoxDigitalIn or SimNpx2RippleModule — the generated overview has 18 OneBox references alone. So the first picture anyone saw of this system on GitHub was months out of date, on a branch named feature/OneBoxSupport.

Its own source admitted the problem in a comment: "HAND-PATCHED RENDER (2026-08-27) … there is no PlantUML or Java in this repo to re-render with." The class diagram carried the same confession in prose: "not regenerated by the build, so treat it as a snapshot." Both were honest, and neither stopped the drift, because a caveat is not a mechanism.

75b. What it is now#

plantuml_export.svg, plantuml_export.txt, architecture.txt and Architecture.pdf are deleted — nothing in this repo can rebuild them, so they could only ever rot.

images/architecture.svg is now a copy of the generated overview, refreshed by scripts/build_docs.py on every build. It stays committed because GitHub renders the README from the repository and a git-ignored file does not appear — but committed is not the same as hand-drawn.

The class diagram is not replaced by another picture. The layered structure prose in images/architecture_overview.md is the maintained form: it describes C++ types, which the pipeline generator does not cover, and prose can at least be checked against the headers.

75c. Why these are NOT dated, when screenshots are#

The operator asked for these to be dated too. They should not be, and the reason is worth writing down because it is the same rule from the other side.

§72 dated the screenshots because a hand-made artifact cannot say how old it is and nothing re-derives it. The architecture diagrams are the opposite: a function of the wiring, re-derived on every build. Dating them would mean a new filename on every build — and stamping a date inside the SVG would be worse still, because make_architecture.py --check compares the file on disk against a fresh render. A date in the image would make that comparison fail every day, destroying the one mechanism that actually guarantees freshness in order to display a claim about it.

Date what cannot be re-derived. Check what can. A date is a promise a human has to keep; a check is one the build keeps. Where both are available the check wins, and adding the date on top breaks it.

python/tests/test_architecture_current.py holds the two halves:
the generated SVGs match what the code would produce right now (--check) proved by adding a config and watching it go red
images/architecture.svg is byte-identical to the generated overview proved by hand-editing the committed copy — the exact historical failure

76. THE ARCHITECTURE DIAGRAM: READABLE, HONEST, AND NOW A SKILL (2026-09-01)#

Operator, looking at the regenerated picture: "should probably be top down rather than left to right … you need to ensure everything fits properly which it does not at all right now", then "data.audio and proc.audio don't connect to anything which just seems off", then "the config row is overly complicated", and finally the one that mattered most: "what you're using to generate the diagram isn't properly documented nor is it an established skill."

76a. It did not fit, for two compounding reasons#

Stages were columns, so the canvas was a function of the BIGGEST stage. With every config in one picture that is ~37 boxes, and the result came out 1580 x 2460 — a tall ribbon four times higher than wide. Stages are bands now and it is 1760 x 1114.

Boxes were a fixed 200 px with centred text and no measurement. Config labels run to 54 characters ("Simulated NPX 2.0 Multishank + Real NI-DAQ + Ripples"), about 270 px at 10 px type — spilling out of both sides and over the neighbours. Widths are computed per node now, clamped, and long names truncate with an ellipsis. There is also a viewBox, without which an SVG renders at intrinsic size and overflows any narrower container.

76b. The floating nodes were real, and the operator found them by looking#

data.audio, proc.audio, data.spike and proc.spike had no edge touching them. Not a rendering artifact: the generator parses config/*.yaml for wiring, and those three processors are auto-provisioned by ProcessorManager in C++, so no profile ever declares their inputs.

proc.lfp    data.npx.raw -> data.lfp      2.0 only (raw, no native lfp)   ProcessorManager.cpp:149-154
proc.spike  data.npx.raw -> data.spike    2.0 only                        same
proc.audio  data.npx.ap else raw -> data.audio   any probe band           ProcessorManager.cpp:202-206
                                                                          AudioProcessor.cpp:913-917

Now supplied by an AUTO_PROVISIONED table and drawn orange and dashed with its own legend entry — "auto-provisioned in C++ (not in the profile)" — because the profile wires this and the program wires this are different claims. It is a second source of truth and is marked as one.

Fixing it exposed a second bug: those edges point upward (a derived-band processor produces data, and Data sits above Processor), and the layout was silently dropping every backward edge as a presumed loop. So the first attempt at showing this wiring hid two thirds of it. Upward edges now route out to the side.

76c. The config band was a hairball#

37 profiles each drawing an independent curve into 9 sources. Every line crossed most of the others and none could be traced, which is the only thing those edges are for. Two changes: configs are ordered by the source they feed (sources are alphabetical, so a profile now sits roughly above its target), and their edges are bundled into a collector point above each source. They are also drawn fainter — at that count they are context, not the subject.

76d. The point the operator actually made#

"I don't think the diagram was blind — what you're using to generate it isn't properly documented nor is it an established skill for you nor future agents."

That is the correct diagnosis. Every failure above was discoverable by anyone who read the generator, and nobody had, because nothing pointed at it: make_architecture.py was referenced in passing by BuildingTheDocs.md and had no page of its own. So the next agent would have rediscovered the same things by looking at the same broken picture.

  • developer_docs/ArchitectureDiagrams.md — what it reads, where the edges come from, the C++-provisioned table and why it is drawn differently, every layout rule with the cost that bought it, how to render an SVG headlessly to actually look at it, and what is committed versus generated. Indexed in developer_docs/README.md.
  • .claude/skills/architecture-diagrams/SKILL.md — loads on "regenerate the architecture diagram", "why is this node not connected to anything", "the README diagram", "make_architecture --check failed". The main spikespeak skill now points at it.

The skill leads with the trap, because it is the one that cost this session: a node with no edges is usually the diagram being blind, not the system being disconnected.

77. ASAP BECOMES A CAPABILITY, NOT A SECOND COPY OF THE PROFILE (2026-09-01, #80)#

Operator: "Go ahead and get the ASAP mode selectable in the GUI … if one module needs it, like the Ripple Detector, then modules it's receiving data from upstream also need it." On a separate branch (feature/AsapPerModule) with its own PR, per their instruction.

77a. Why a config key was the wrong shape#

low_latency was readable only from YAML, at connect, with no GUI control anywhere. So the only way to run a closed loop at low latency was to select a different profile — which is why five files exist whose entire content is one flipped boolean, and why the matrix is incomplete by construction: 11 hardware profiles present, 9 combinations missing. Encoding orthogonal flags as filenames costs 2^n files, so it can never be finished.

77b. Three measurements that shaped the design#

ASAP changes exactly one thing. mTimer->setInterval(mLowLatency ? 0 : 1) and nothing else. Nothing is allocated or sized by it, and the worker runs at QThread::HighPriority in both modes — it is a high-priority spin, not a priority change. Cost: ~98 % of one core against 13.4 %.

The flag is main-thread-only and read once, in startAcq, and the probe worker is created fresh on every Play. So a plain setter gives pause → toggle → play with no synchronisation, no worker and no vendor call touched. That is why the GUI control locks while acquiring rather than pretending to apply live.

The processors are already ASAP, and this is the finding that made propagation honest rather than theatrical. BandFilterProcessor and RippleDetector both drive off waitForAvailable(consumerId, 1, available) — whose second argument is a timeout in milliseconds, not a minimum count. They wake on the first sample and take whatever is there. Block size, and therefore latency, is set by the source's commit cadence and inherited downstream, exactly as LatencyProfiling.md concluded when the source flag removed the ~400 µs detector backlog.

So: a consumer asks the data path; each producer either has a knob and uses it, or reports that it is already minimal. Only sources have a knob today. A processor that later batches gets the request for free, and the module needing the latency never has to know which ancestor can act.

77c. Nothing could walk the graph#

IProcessor exposed only configure/start/stop. IDataSource reported no outputs. ProcessorManager stores name -> (IProcessor*, QThread*) with no edges. Which is precisely why the one propagation that already existed — RippleDetector::ensureInputBandEnabled — is a hardcoded single hop keyed on the literal "data.lfp".

Modules now report their edges. Both new mixins have all-defaulted methods, so every existing module compiles untouched and opts in only if it has something to say.

77d. Who declares what, which is the part the operator cared about#

module declares why
source.npx.*, both sims supports + exposes the only real knob; on a rig, "how stale is the sample I am reacting to" is an operator question
proc.lfp edges only already ASAP; walked through to reach the source, and deliberately given no control — it is in the path because the detector needs it, not because anyone should be tuning it
proc.rippledetector exposes, not supports nothing of its own to tune, but it is where the operator decides

exposesLowLatency() being separate from supportsLowLatency() is the whole mechanism for "present in the GUI at the discretion of that module's developer".

77e. Ownership, and a test expectation of mine that was wrong#

Release gives back only what the request took. The end-to-end check failed at first on "the source turned off" — and the code was right, the assertion was wrong: SimNpx2RippleModule.yaml sets low_latency: true, so the source was already in ASAP before anything was asked (pending=True on the very first dump). The request correctly took no ownership, and release correctly left the profile's setting alone. Corrected to assert that, which is a more valuable check than the one originally written — and the inverse of the bug where one ripple run left proc.lfp filtering 384 channels for a whole session.

77f. Two things found along the way#

markProvisioned() is called by only two processors — BandFilterProcessor and AudioProcessor. Filtering the graph on isProvisioned() therefore returned an EMPTY graph and made the ripple detector invisible. Membership in mProcessorDatas is "in this graph"; isProvisioned exists for the registry path, which this does not use.

The sim sources got the capability too, deliberately: every GUI test runs against simulated sources, so a capability the sim cannot express is one nothing can test without a probe on the bench.

77g. Verified#

  • tests/test_lowlatencygraph.cpp — the NP2 chain reaches the source two hops away through a knobless node; NP1 is one hop shorter through the same code; release returns only what it took; a module reporting no edges is safely skipped; a cycle terminates. Proved by reverting the walk to a single hop and watching it go red.
  • tests/gui/low_latency.py — who is offered a control, propagation, the lock while acquiring, and confirmed state (pending → "takes effect on the next Play" → active after a Play).
  • A live end-to-end run over the shipped WebSocket command: proc.rippledetector → proc.lfp → source.npx.sim2, request accepted, source picked it up, a module that does not expose it refused with a reason rather than silently doing nothing.

78. THE AGREED QUEUE (2026-09-01, live)#

Recorded here as well as in the machine-local agent memory, because that memory does not cross machines and item 6 happens on a different box.

# item state
1 ~~#80 ASAP — gate green, commit + push~~ ✅ DONE 2026-09-01 — 757a5a2, gate 27/140/24
2 ~~PR for #80 → merge, delete the branch~~ ✅ DONE — PR #81 MERGED (fast-forward), branch deleted, #80 closed
3 ~~"below threshold" label → "no detection"~~ ✅ DONE — 771b88f
4 ~~The dock renders four defaults it does not own~~ ✅ DONE — 771b88f
5 Gate speedup — --jobs 4 twice; flip the default if green twice. Also make run_gui_tests.py take the gate lock 🔴 in progress
6 Linux run — the operator runs Configuration 1. The last item on #52 deferred by the operator until the gate hang is fixed
7 Autodetect with NO probe attached — OneBox and PXIe basestation both connected to one machine, neither holding a probe. A state the rig has never been in before. Read the code before touching the hardware. Extends to: Intan-over-Ethernet later behaves the same (detect its presence, do nothing if nothing is attached), and if all three DO have probes the operator is prompted to choose — unless a custom config names one to autoconnect 🔴 requested 2026-09-01, hardware is set up NOW
8 Configs: confirm the consolidation actually landed and that both the YAML set and its docs stay simple. The users are neuroscientists — the number of selectable configs, and the docs describing them, must not be developer-shaped 🔴 requested 2026-09-01

Order matters. 3 and 4 both edit webinterface/RippleModule.js, which the #80 branch also modified, so they wait until after the merge.

Two operator decisions already taken — do not re-ask. The column label becomes "no detection", which states the confirmed backend fact and makes no claim about the signal, so it cannot contradict the sparkline drawn directly above it. A newly added channel gets z = 5.0, matching the detector's own mZScoreThreshold; this makes new channels less sensitive than before, which was accepted deliberately.

Item 5, so it is not re-investigated from scratch. The parallel path works and measured 1.46× (1255 s against 1827 s), but one test used to hang to the 900 s subprocess timeout and which test changed between runs. Port collision is ruled out — ensure_backend fails in ~30 s with a clear error rather than hanging. The CDP per-recv and per-call deadlines that would bound this landed in the same commit as the serial default (472d71b), so git cannot say whether the hang predates them. So the cheap move is simply to re-run it twice, which is this file's own stated criterion.

Also found while answering "can I start the queued tasks while a gate runs": run_gui_tests.py:160 reuses a backend it did not start (if jobs == 1 and not backend_up()), probing the default ports. The full gate takes a system-wide lock and the GUI runner does not, so a second suite launched beside a running gate silently attaches to the gate's backend — two suites driving one backend, which is exactly the failure that once looked like a regression. Folded into item 5.

79. THE GATE HANG WAS ONE WAIT WITH NO DEADLINE (2026-09-01, item 5)#

The GUI suite ran serially because of a recorded mystery: at --jobs > 1 "one test hangs to the 900 s subprocess timeout and which test it is changes between runs." An unreliable fast gate being worse than a reliable slow one, the default stayed at 1 and every gate paid ~17 extra minutes.

It was not flakiness. It was one unbounded wait and a reporter that destroyed the evidence.

What was actually happening#

A --jobs 4 run was caught live, mid-hang, and taken apart:

  1. A backend wedges. Process alive, socket listening, TCP connections accepted, 0 % CPU, and answering nothing on either its WebSocket or its HTTP port. A bounded probe confirmed it: both ports accept and never reply.
  2. Teardown waits forever. spikespeak_cli.WSClient.connect called settimeout(None) on the line after create_connection and before reading the handshake reply, so its timeout argument bounded only the TCP connect. stream_options was found holding an ESTABLISHED socket to a backend that would never answer. The OS accepts on a listening socket whether or not the application behind it still runs its event loop, so "connected" never implied "alive."
  3. The report is destroyed. run_gui_tests.py passed timeout=900 and never caught TimeoutExpired. The exception escaped the worker, pool.map, and main() — so one hung test printed a traceback, wrote no gui_test_status.json, and discarded the pass/fail of all 26 tests. That is precisely why nobody could say which test hung: the runner could not report it.
  4. Six backends leaked. Teardown sent quit, slept 4 s, and never looked at whether the process exited — this repo's recurring defect (#16/#18, #51) sitting in the teardown path: a REQUEST treated as a confirmed outcome. Worse, self._backend is the launcher; the real binary is its child (spikespeak.py:376), so even terminate() would have orphaned the app — the identical wrapper-script trap already documented at the Chrome launch, and true on Windows too.

The hang always struck in teardown, after the test's assertions had already passed. The suite was never testing anything wrong; it was failing to hang up the phone.

The --porcelain branch had no timeout at all#

Worse than the parallel path, and it is the gate's own path. It iterates the child's stdout, which blocks forever on a hung child, and only reaches child.wait(timeout=…) once the pipe closes, which a hung child never does. A wedged backend could have hung run_all_tests.py indefinitely.

What changed#
spikespeak_cli.WSClient.connect the deadline covers the whole handshake; settimeout(None) moved below the read loop, since unbounded is right for streaming and never right for being greeted
tests/gui/_harness.py teardown confirms the backend died, and kills the tree (launcher and binary) on both platforms; POSIX gets its own process group, matching the Chrome launch
scripts/run_gui_tests.py TimeoutExpired is caught and reported as a failed test; the porcelain branch gets a watchdog; the deadline is SPIKESPEAK_GUI_TEST_TIMEOUT, overridable so the timeout path is testable

Proof, per CLAUDE.md#

python/tests/test_gui_runner_timeout.py, 5 cases, each proved able to fail by injecting the real regression: settimeout(None) moved back above the read loop → red; the TimeoutExpired handler removed → red, reproducing the original symptom verbatim (GUI test runner crashed: TimeoutExpired(...), no results); the watchdog disarmed → red. A separate live check drove a genuinely wedged server that accepts and never answers — the exact 2026-09-01 condition — and confirmed the client now raises in 3.0 s instead of never.

Two lessons worth keeping. A deadline nobody can trigger on purpose is a deadline nobody has ever seen work — hence the env override. And the first version of the source-level guard failed on the docstring of the function it was checking, which explains the bug and so contains the forbidden string; it now reads the AST. A source guard that greps text is a guard against writing prose.

Still open — the wedge itself#

These changes make a wedged backend survivable and reported; they do not explain why a backend stops servicing its event loop while its socket stays open. Since it strikes at teardown, after the assertions pass, bounding the wait converts it from a lost run into a clean one — but the wedge is a real backend defect and deserves its own issue.

80. AUTODETECT WITH TWO BASESTATIONS AND NO PROBE — READ FROM THE CODE (2026-09-01, item 7)#

The operator connected a OneBox and a PXIe basestation to one machine with no probe on either — a state the rig has never been in — and asked whether it is handled, from the code, before anything is run against it. It is handled in part. The gap is specific and worth stating exactly.

What already works, and why it is safe to plug both in#

Npx::basestations() calls only np_scanBS() and np_getDeviceList(). Neither opens a basestation; np_openBS() does, and that is where the exclusive claim — and the FT601 handle — begin. So "what is plugged in" is answerable for free, before anything is claimed, and having both attached cannot deny either to another process. The scan is cached, because a vendor call on the main thread is a way to lose the Qt event loop, and rigs are not hot-plugged mid-session.

With both attached, resolveAutoSource() returns ok = false and:

A OneBox and a PXIe basestation are both attached (1 PXIe module and 1 OneBox). Only one can be used at a time — select the OneBox or PXIe profile explicitly.

WebSocketServer then broadcasts basestation-choice with __BASESTATIONS__:OneBox,PXIe — a choice, not an error, exactly as the operator asked for. A custom config that names source.npx.onebox or source.npx.pxi directly never reaches source.npx.auto, so autoconnect already works. apiAvailable keeps "this build cannot look" distinct from "nothing is attached", which are different problems for the operator. tests/test_basestationscan.cpp already covers the 1-OneBox-plus-1-PXIe case as ambiguous.

The gap: there is no concept of a probe anywhere in the scan#

BasestationInfo is {isOneBox, id}. Nothing reports whether a headstage or probe is attached. np_scanBS/np_getDeviceList do not carry it; that needs np_openBS and then np_openPort / np_detectHeadStage — the claim the scan deliberately avoids. Two consequences:

  1. In the operator's exact current state — both attached, neither with a probe — the prompt still appears, offering a choice between two basestations that cannot stream. Whichever is picked fails later, at open time, with an unrelated message. The right answer is "two basestations, no probe on either — attach one", and the code cannot currently say it.
  2. The reverse case is worse. If the OneBox has a probe and the PXIe does not, there is an unambiguous correct answer and the code still refuses, because it counts transports, not probes. That is a prompt where none is needed.

Intan-over-Ethernet does not exist yet (tests/gui/intan_not_implemented.py), and the prompt string is the hardcoded "OneBox,PXIe" — so adding a third transport means building that list from what was actually found rather than appending to a literal.

The design question, which is the operator's to answer#

Probe detection costs an np_openBS — the exclusive claim, and on a real rig the thing that must be released cleanly. Detection therefore stops being free, and this repo's standing rule is that a control-plane probe is not a liveness test (#59). The options are a genuine trade:

cost risk
Leave as is none the operator is prompted when there is nothing to choose between, and prompted again when there is nothing to decide
Open, detect, close, during the scan a claim per basestation at scan time must be released cleanly on every path; a wedged basestation now sits in the detection path, not just the connect path
Only when ambiguous a claim only in the 2-basestation case the same risk, confined to the case that actually needs it, and it is the case the operator hit today

The third is the narrowest and resolves both consequences above: with both attached, ask each whether it holds a probe, then either pick the only viable one or say plainly that neither has one.

Not implemented, and deliberately not run against the rig unattended — it changes what the software does to a physically connected basestation, so it wants the operator present. The pure decision function is where the logic belongs (it is already a pure function with its own tests), so the probe-aware table can be written and tested with no hardware at all, and only the scan itself needs the rig.

81. THE CONFIG SET, MEASURED RATHER THAN ASSUMED (2026-09-01, item 8)#

The concern was that "every single combination results in a different configuration file" and that a neuroscientist would meet a wall of them. Counting what an operator actually SEES gives a different picture from counting files.

36 files. 19 are gui_hidden (script- and CLI-only). 17 are offered, and 14 of those are SIMULATIONS. The operator-facing hardware list is two entries plus a placeholder:

offered what it is
Neuropixels source.npx.auto — any basestation, any probe, both read from the rig
PXIe with NI-DAQ the same, plus the NI-DAQ
Intan (MicroZed) placeholder, not implemented

OneBox, PXIe and PXIe + NI-DAQ are deliberately hidden explicit escapes, "still selectable by name" — which is exactly what the ambiguity prompt tells the operator to pick, and what a custom config uses to autoconnect. Verified end to end: the prompt's buttons send selectSource "OneBox" / "PXIe", which match those files' internal name: fields, and gui_hidden suppresses only listing, never selection. So the hardware consolidation landed and works. The remaining complexity is in the SIMULATION picker (14), not the hardware one (2).

The duplication that IS still real, and now provably harmful#

Four *Asap.yaml files exist solely because low_latency used to be readable only from a file:

Npx1OneBoxAsap   Npx1PxiNiDaqAsap   Npx2OneBoxAsap   Npx2PxiNiDaqAsap

Each header states the invariant its purpose depends on — "Identical in every respect EXCEPT low_latency: true … quoting an ASAP-vs-default comparison requires the two configs to differ by this key ALONE."

Three honour it. Npx2OneBoxAsap does not: it is missing proc.audio entirely. Git says why, and it is the textbook failure of flags-as-filenames: proc.audio was added to the base by feature commit 1a9572e and never mirrored into the copy. So an A/B run with that pair differs in two ways while the file swears it differs in one — and the file is read by ripple_tools/fair_ab.py, which exists to produce published latency numbers.

#80 removed the reason these files exist. ASAP is now a runtime control that reports CONFIRMED state (active vs pending), which is better evidence than trusting a filename — a toggle can be verified in force; a file can only be assumed to have been loaded.

Deliberately not deleted in this pass. They are referenced by ripple_tools/fair_ab.py, ripple_tools/run_latency_hw.py, python/tests/test_config_channels.py and two user_docs pages. Collapsing them means changing tooling whose output is published latency data, on hardware, and the right sequence is to switch that tooling to set the flag at runtime and assert it took before the files go. Doing it blind, overnight, on measurement code is how a silently wrong number gets published. Queued with the evidence above so the next pass is mechanical.

82. THE BACKEND IGNORES quit FAR MORE OFTEN THAN ANYONE KNEW (2026-09-01, item 5 cont.)#

§79 made a wedged backend survivable. Making it VISIBLE then showed the wedge is not rare at all.

Once the runner started reporting surviving backends — nothing had ever checked, despite "nothing left running" being on the hand-back checklist — a green run turned out to be leaking:

✅ ALL 26 GUI TESTS PASSED
❌ 1 backend process(es) SURVIVED this run

And once a PASSING test's teardown warnings were surfaced too (the parallel path discards a passing test's output, so the harness's own messages went nowhere), the scale became clear:

8 of 26 tests had a backend ignore quit outright. band_dependency, error_paths, heatmap_view, layout_matrix, low_latency, probe_map (twice), ripple_detach.

Every one of them stops at the same place in its own log — Quit requested via control command. → Tearing down source... → and then nothing, where a healthy shutdown continues to WebSocket server destructor executing. This is a backend defect, not a test defect, and the suite is simply the first thing that ever exercised shutdown 26 times in ten minutes.

It is NOT the obvious candidate: quit with no source selected, quit after a failed profile, and quit after a good source plus Disconnect were each driven directly and all exited cleanly in 0.7 s. The untested suspect is the race the harness creates — Chrome is killed first, which starts the backend's own "last client gone, quitting in 2000 ms" timer, and then a NEW client connects and sends quit. Two shutdown paths, and only under load. Unproven, and it deserves its own issue.

Why two still leaked, and the fix that generalises#

Every teardown path confirmed a proxy rather than the outcome:

path what it confirmed what it missed
self._backend.wait() the launcher exited the binary it started is a separate process
taskkill /T a signal was sent to a tree a child mid-shutdown can be reparented and survive
the whole block — it is skipped entirely when ensure_backend() reused a backend (proc = None)

Three routes, all seen. So rather than patch each, teardown now checks the fact that actually matters — is anything still running on THIS test's ports — and kills it, whatever route left it there. Command-line matching keeps it to tools that exist everywhere (PowerShell, pgrep) with no new dependency, and it cannot touch another test's backend because every worker owns its port pair.

And a fourth route was found by reading rather than by failing: Python does not call __exit__ when __enter__ raises — the context was never entered. Gui.__enter__ starts a backend on its first line and has several raise sites after it (source never ready, Chrome died, debug port never opened), so any of them leaked a backend and a browser with no message at all. __enter__ is now wrapped: it tears down whatever it had built, then re-raises the original error unchanged.

The theme of this whole day, stated once: confirm the thing you care about, not a proxy for it. An accepted TCP connection is not a live application; an exited launcher is not an exited backend; a signalled tree is not a dead tree; and a passing test is not a clean one.

The last leak: there were TWO teardowns, and only one was fixed#

Runs 4 and 5 stayed green and still leaked exactly one backend. The cause was not the escalation — it was that four tests never use Gui at all: command_surface, intan_not_implemented, state_consistency and teardown_while_recording call ensure_backend() directly and hand-rolled their own teardown, which sent quit, slept, and never checked. Since the backend ignores quit often enough to hit several tests per run, those four leaked a wedged process on any run that caught them, and nothing said so.

Fixing Gui.__exit__ could never have fixed them. Two teardowns means fixing one and still leaking, so there is now one — _harness.stop_backend() — used by Gui.__exit__ and by all four direct callers. It sends quit (bounded), confirms the process died, kills the tree if not, and then always checks the port, since an exited launcher is not an exited backend.

Two smaller corrections found on the way, both instances of the same habit:

  • the port check first matched every process's command line for --ws-port N. That enumerated the whole process table — four workers doing that at once under load is exactly when it is slowest — and it matched the helper process running the query, whose own command line contains the port number. Reading the port table instead is 25× faster (0.012 s vs 0.296 s), does not degrade with process count, and answers the question actually being asked: not who was started with this number in their arguments but who is holding this socket.
  • that check also swallowed every exception and returned, so a query that timed out under load was indistinguishable from a clean teardown. A check that fails quietly is not a check; it is a place where the answer is always "nothing to do". It now says so.

83. THE WEDGE, FOUND AND FIXED: A RE-ENTRANT DEADLOCK ON SHUTDOWN (2026-09-01)#

§82 left the backend wedge open and called it "a real backend defect deserving its own issue." It is now closed, and the mechanism is exact.

Three hypotheses falsified before the right move#

Each was tested rather than argued, and each was wrong:

hypothesis result
quit hangs when no source is set No. No source, bad profile, and good-source-plus-Disconnect each exited in 0.7 s
the 2 s auto-quit timer racing an explicit quit (the operator's) No. 0/4 and 0/3 wedged. With a 3 s gap the quit got ConnectionRefusedError — the timer had fired and the app had already exited cleanly
an abruptly-killed client leaving a half-open socket No. 0/3. Even with the client taskkilled, the backend noticed and exited

Three experiments, no wedge. The right move was to stop guessing and read a stack: catch a wedged process alive (a temporary harness flag) and attach cdb non-invasively.

What the stack said#

Qt6Core!QTimer::timeout                       the deferred quit fires
  SpikesPeak+0x1196f
    Qt6Core!QThread::terminate                terminate() is called
      Qt6Core!QThread::finished               ...which emits finished() SYNCHRONOUSLY
        Qt6Gui!...processApplicationTermination
          SpikesPeak+0x11961
            Qt6Core!QThread::isRunning        the handler calls back into the SAME QThread
              Qt6Core!QBasicMutex::lockInternal
                KERNELBASE!WaitOnAddress      blocked forever, on the MAIN THREAD

Two nested processApplicationTermination frames — the re-entry, visible in the stack.

The cycle, in src/main.cpp#

connect(kbListenerThread, &QThread::finished, &a, &QApplication::quit);   // :568
connect(&a, &QCoreApplication::aboutToQuit, [kbListenerThread]() {
    if (kbListenerThread && kbListenerThread->isRunning()) {              // :582
        kbListenerThread->terminate();

quit → aboutToQuit → terminate() → the thread ends, so finished is emitted from inside terminate() while the QThread's mutex is held → :568 calls QApplication::quit again → aboutToQuit re-enters → isRunning() wants that same mutex → the main thread blocks forever. The process stays alive, keeps its ports, and answers nothing — which is exactly the wedge.

Why it was intermittent, and why nobody found it before the suite ran 26 shutdowns in ten minutes: when stdin is closed — every headless and test run — the listener's getline returns and the thread ends by itself, so isRunning() is usually already false and nothing is terminated. It deadlocks only when the app quits before that has happened. A startup race, widened by load: 8-10 of 26 tests per run, never the same ones, only in parallel.

The fix, and the proof#

The aboutToQuit handler runs once. The second, re-entrant pass returns before touching the QThread, so the mutex is never taken from inside terminate(). A flag rather than a disconnect: the second pass has nothing to do either way, and it stays correct if another aboutToQuit path is added.

Measured, same suite, same machine, same --jobs 4:

backends that ignored quit leaked
before 8-10 of 26, every run 1-2 per run
after 0 0

The harness hardening from §79 and §82 stays: it is what made this findable, and a backend that wedges for some other reason is still bounded, killed and reported rather than hanging the gate.

84. THE LINUX GATE'S LEFTOVERS, CLOSED — AND THE MERGE (2026-09-01)#

The 2026-09-01 Linux run fixed its two blockers and flagged four more, three of them explicitly "not fixed here". All are now closed, feature/OneBoxSupport is merged into develop (fast-forward, 60889d7), and every fix carries a test that was proved able to fail by injecting the real regression.

What was fixed, and the one that matters most#

Nothing guarded the cross-platform arch contract. That is the interesting one, because it is a shape Windows structurally cannot catch: linux/HostAudio.hpp had drifted from both the Windows header and its own .cpp, the Linux .cpp still compiled — it was defining a function its own header never declared, which is legal — and MSVC never compiles that header at all. python/tests/test_arch_parity.py asserts both directions (all platforms agree; each platform matches its own .cpp) and catches the exact regression from Windows in 3 ms with no compiler. The second invariant is the one that would have caught it alone: the Linux .cpp agreed with the Windows header all along.

The other three: the EYE is now a per-stream publish interval; rig.py joins its reader before closing the file it writes (and set_capture(), where the same defect silently killed the reader and lost every later detection); and layout_matrix reports UNTESTED as a result rather than passing vacuously. A clean clone no longer fails on a missing build step.

Three lessons, all versions of the same one#

A pure function fed pre-digested data pins nothing. The first EYE fix had the page group frames by stream and a pure reducer merely average them. That looks testable and is not: fed per-stream data, no implementation can pool by accident. Moving the grouping into Python — the place the defect actually lived — is what let a test feed the real interleaved burst and watch the old implementation go red.

Two of my own first-cut tests were vacuous, and only injection found them. The rig.py close test asserted "no exception escaped the reader", which stayed green against the true original code on this machine — it depended on a frame landing inside the race window. Rewritten to instrument the handle and record whether the reader was still alive when something closed it, it is deterministic and cannot be dodged by timing. The set_capture test had the same weakness and got the same treatment.

And I asserted a proxy while fixing proxy-assertions. Applying five edits to rig.py, I checked assert s != orig — which proves something changed, not that all five did. Edit 4 silently failed to match and I only noticed because a test went red. The check should have been per-edit, and now is.

The record, corrected#

Testing.md disagreed with itself: the Linux run updated the headline totals but left the Python section reading "Six files, 126 tests" against an actual seventeen files. Now derived from a run. That table has been wrong twice in the same way, and its own closing sentence explains why — an inventory understates a suite by precisely the thing it omits.

7e2b2fa is also better verified than the 2026-08-26 caveat implied. It is an ancestor of the Config 4 commit, so the PXIe rig session ran with it in the tree — and band_dependency.py opens two Gui sessions in one backend process, which is the stale-subscriber condition, so every gate run exercises it on both platforms. Configuration 3 remains the genuinely unexercised row.

Operator decisions recorded#

  • Headless / CI does not matter. The GUI exists to be looked at; a real X session and a real GPU is the supported configuration. It should stop being listed as an open risk.
  • OneBox support is complete, and the branch is merged.

85. THE 2026-08-28 IN-VIVO SESSION — IT RAN, AND IT WAS NOISY (recorded 2026-09-02)#

Recorded from the operator, because this repository had no record of it at all — and the absence caused a real error. §65 is titled "THE MORNING OF AN IN-VIVO SESSION" and documents the fixes made in the hours before it; nothing anywhere recorded what happened next. On 2026-09-01 a documentation pass read user_docs/README.md's claim that the rig "went in vivo under anesthesia on 2026-08-28", could not support it from §65 or anywhere else, and correctly refused to leave an unverifiable claim about an animal experiment on an operator-facing page — but then under-stated it in the other direction, saying no results were recorded. The session had run.

What actually happened#

The session ran on 2026-08-28. The recording was noisy and required a lot of filtering — not unusable, and it should not be described that way. The rig has been cleaned up considerably since. More in-vivo data is expected the week of 2026-09-07.

Worth having written down, because two adjacent facts are easy to conflate: every closed-loop figure this project quotes is still a saline bench number, and the reason is not that in vivo was never attempted. It was, and it worked; it simply did not add rows to the latency matrix. A reader who assumes the first will draw the wrong conclusion about what remains to be proven.

The lesson, which is not about this session#

A milestone that is planned, then reached, then not recorded, decays into a claim nobody can check. The banner named "in-vivo under anesthesia" as the next milestone; §65 recorded the morning of the day it happened; user_docs/README.md then asserted the milestone as achieved and attributed it to §65, which does not say that. Every step was reasonable and the end state was a user-facing claim with no support behind it.

A session gets a Status entry on the day, even when — especially when — it produced nothing. A null result is a result, and it is the one most likely to go unrecorded, because there are no numbers to paste. Had this one been written down, the doc pass would have found it and none of this would have needed correcting twice.
